Patent Law English

Patent-practice English for invention interviews, prior-art discussions, claims, drafting support, office actions, international filing, freedom-to-operate reviews, and licensing.

For patent attorneys, patent agents, intellectual-property specialists, invention reviewers.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Elicit the mechanism, alternatives, and individual contributions behind an invention disclosure.
  • Explain a search result using feature mapping, dates, and the limits of the search scope.
  • Translate claim relationships into plain English without changing their scope.
  • Distinguish filed disclosure from later engineering developments when discussing an amendment.
  • Separate the examiner position, proposed response, and procedural status in a client update.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. Language practice, not patent or legal advice. Examples simplify documents and do not establish inventorship, patentability, validity, infringement, priority, filing deadlines, licensing rights, or freedom to operate. Real matters require qualified patent professionals, current law and office practice, and the actual documents. Protect confidential inventions and observe applicable filing, disclosure, security, and export requirements.

01Interviewing inventors without leading their answersThe improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling+

Your goal

Elicit the mechanism, alternatives, and individual contributions behind an invention disclosure.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling

Engineer Amira describes a prototype battery enclosure with a curved coolant channel. Technician Leo built it from drawings and suggested a detachable connector. One internal test reports a shorter cooling time, but the test conditions have not been compared. Patent professional Daniel is gathering facts, not deciding inventorship or patentability.

Amira: Engineer / Daniel: Patent professional

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What change is described in the enclosure?

Answer and explanation

A. A curved coolant channel

The supplied mechanism concerns a curved coolant channel in the prototype enclosure.

2. What is known about Leo?

Answer and explanation

B. He built the prototype and suggested a connector

The brief identifies construction and a connector suggestion without deciding inventorship.

3. What limits the cooling-time comparison?

Answer and explanation

D. The test conditions have not been compared

A single internal result cannot establish a controlled comparison without checking conditions.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Describe each contribution before assessing its legal significance. Building a prototype and conceiving a claimed feature are not interchangeable descriptions.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
invention disclosure
Internal account of a proposed invention and its development.submit an invention disclosure
technical problem
Practical technical difficulty the proposed invention addresses.define the technical problem
technical effect
Technical result attributed to a feature or process.substantiate a technical effect
conception
Formation of the inventive idea under the applicable legal test.investigate contribution to conception
reduction to practice
Implementation or legally relevant realization of an invention.document reduction to practice
inventorship
Legal identification of the inventor or inventors.assess inventorship
contribution record
Account of who contributed what and when.preserve the contribution record
prototype
Preliminary implementation used to explore or test a design.evaluate the prototype
embodiment
Particular implementation described for an invention.describe an embodiment
alternative configuration
Different arrangement of components or steps.disclose an alternative configuration
coolant channel
Passage through which cooling fluid moves.trace the coolant channel
detachable connector
Connection component designed to be separated.specify a detachable connector
causal mechanism
Process explaining how a feature produces a result.explain the causal mechanism
comparative test
Test comparing implementations under stated conditions.conduct a comparative test
baseline configuration
Reference arrangement used for comparison.identify the baseline configuration
laboratory notebook
Contemporaneous record of experimental work.preserve the laboratory notebook
confidential disclosure
Communication within the relevant confidentiality arrangements.control confidential disclosure
public disclosure
Making information available outside the relevant confidential setting.review public disclosure
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

An inventor interview gathers facts. Seniority does not establish inventorship, and a promising test result does not establish patentability.

C Notice the language

Curiosity before characterization

Ask about contributions and evidence without assigning legal labels based on seniority, job title, or who presents the invention.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Elicit the mechanism
What does the curved channel change in the flow path?
Probe an alternative
Would the same effect occur with a straight channel?
Trace a contribution
Who first proposed that feature, and what records support the account?
Separate result from explanation
The test reports a shorter time; it does not yet establish why.
Clarify implementation
Did Leo follow the drawings or propose a different arrangement?
Preserve uncertainty
We have not compared the test conditions yet.
Protect disclosure
Please use the approved confidential channel for the drawings.
Summarize evidence
We have a prototype, a reported result, and contribution details to verify.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Designed versus built
Designed concerns the arrangement; built concerns implementation. A person may have done either or both.
Because versus after
Because asserts causation; after identifies sequence without establishing the cause.
Could versus did
Could describes a possible alternative; did describes what actually happened.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question is least leading?

Answer and explanation

B. Who proposed the connector, and when?

The neutral question elicits contribution facts without suggesting the desired legal conclusion.

2. Which test summary is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. A shorter cooling time was reported in one test

The brief supplies a reported result but no verified causal or controlled comparison.

3. What should the interview capture about alternatives?

Answer and explanation

A. How a straight channel would change the mechanism

Explaining alternatives identifies technical relationships without inventing evidence or legal conclusions.

4. Which status is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Inventorship remains for qualified assessment

The interview gathers facts without establishing the applicable legal inventorship outcome.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Amira: Engineer / Daniel: Patent professional

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • inventorship
  • public disclosure
  • contribution record
  • laboratory notebook
  • invention disclosure
  • alternative configuration
  • causal mechanism
  • technical problem
  • embodiment
  • baseline configuration
  1. Amira
    Our enclosure cools faster. I would like the application to focus on that benefit rather than the shape of the tubing.
  2. Daniel
    Let us identify the 1 first. What limits cooling in the reference design, and which feature changes that behavior?
  3. Amira
    Fluid bypasses part of the enclosure. The curved channel takes it around that area instead of directly to the outlet.
  4. Daniel
    That describes a 2. We should distinguish that explanation from the measurements supporting it.
  5. Amira
    One internal test showed a shorter cooling time. I have not checked whether both tests used the same starting temperature.
  6. Daniel
    Then we need the 3 and test conditions before describing the result as a controlled improvement.
  7. Amira
    Leo built the enclosure from my drawings, but he suggested making the connector detachable for easier assembly.
  8. Daniel
    Record that in the 4. Construction work and a new design suggestion should not be collapsed into one description.
  9. Amira
    Should I list only senior engineers as inventors? Leo is a technician and did not present the project to management.
  10. Daniel
    Job title does not settle 5. We need the relevant facts and proposed claims before reaching that conclusion.
  11. Amira
    I also sketched a straight channel with a baffle, although we have not built that version yet.
  12. Daniel
    Describe the 6 and explain whether you expect it to address the same flow problem.
  13. Amira
    The curved version is the one we built. The straight version remains a drawing, with no performance measurements.
  14. Daniel
    Identify each 7 separately and avoid implying that the untested version produced the reported data.
  15. Amira
    The notebook contains dated sketches and build notes. I can collect those without rewriting the original entries.
  16. Daniel
    Preserve the 8 and provide copies through the approved confidential process.
  17. Amira
    Marketing wants to show the prototype at a public event next month. I have not approved that request.
  18. Daniel
    Flag the proposed 9 for prompt legal review before anyone assumes the timing is harmless.
  19. Amira
    I will supply the drawings, contribution details, test conditions, and event request as separate items for your review.
  20. Daniel
    That gives the 10 a factual foundation without promising protection or treating an initial result as a verified product claim.

Answers and explanations
  1. technical problem Turn 2. The technical problem identifies the difficulty addressed rather than substituting a marketing benefit for the mechanism.
  2. causal mechanism Turn 4. The causal mechanism connects the arrangement to the proposed effect while leaving performance evidence open to review.
  3. baseline configuration Turn 6. The baseline configuration is the reference arrangement needed to interpret the comparison rather than assuming comparable conditions.
  4. contribution record Turn 8. The contribution record preserves who proposed each feature without prematurely deciding the legal inventorship question.
  5. inventorship Turn 10. Inventorship requires the applicable legal assessment of contributions, not a ranking by seniority or presentation role.
  6. alternative configuration Turn 12. An alternative configuration is another arrangement that should be identified accurately, including whether it was constructed or tested.
  7. embodiment Turn 14. An embodiment is a particular implementation, so the tested version must remain distinct from an untested proposed arrangement.
  8. laboratory notebook Turn 16. The laboratory notebook contains contemporaneous development evidence and should be preserved rather than rewritten to fit a preferred account.
  9. public disclosure Turn 18. Public disclosure can have patent consequences under actual law and facts, so the plan requires prompt qualified review.
  10. invention disclosure Turn 20. The invention disclosure organizes the technical facts and development history without itself establishing patentability or inventorship.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Amira: Our enclosure cools faster. I would like the application to focus on that benefit rather than the shape of the tubing.
  2. Daniel: Let us identify the technical problem first. What limits cooling in the reference design, and which feature changes that behavior?
  3. Amira: Fluid bypasses part of the enclosure. The curved channel takes it around that area instead of directly to the outlet.
  4. Daniel: That describes a causal mechanism. We should distinguish that explanation from the measurements supporting it.
  5. Amira: One internal test showed a shorter cooling time. I have not checked whether both tests used the same starting temperature.
  6. Daniel: Then we need the baseline configuration and test conditions before describing the result as a controlled improvement.
  7. Amira: Leo built the enclosure from my drawings, but he suggested making the connector detachable for easier assembly.
  8. Daniel: Record that in the contribution record. Construction work and a new design suggestion should not be collapsed into one description.
  9. Amira: Should I list only senior engineers as inventors? Leo is a technician and did not present the project to management.
  10. Daniel: Job title does not settle inventorship. We need the relevant facts and proposed claims before reaching that conclusion.
  11. Amira: I also sketched a straight channel with a baffle, although we have not built that version yet.
  12. Daniel: Describe the alternative configuration and explain whether you expect it to address the same flow problem.
  13. Amira: The curved version is the one we built. The straight version remains a drawing, with no performance measurements.
  14. Daniel: Identify each embodiment separately and avoid implying that the untested version produced the reported data.
  15. Amira: The notebook contains dated sketches and build notes. I can collect those without rewriting the original entries.
  16. Daniel: Preserve the laboratory notebook and provide copies through the approved confidential process.
  17. Amira: Marketing wants to show the prototype at a public event next month. I have not approved that request.
  18. Daniel: Flag the proposed public disclosure for prompt legal review before anyone assumes the timing is harmless.
  19. Amira: I will supply the drawings, contribution details, test conditions, and event request as separate items for your review.
  20. Daniel: That gives the invention disclosure a factual foundation without promising protection or treating an initial result as a verified product claim.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The test engineer also proposed a feature

Counsel checks development contributions.

  1. Counsel: The project report calls Dana a tester. Did Dana also propose any changes to the design?
  2. Engineer: Yes. Dana suggested moving the inlet after observing uneven flow during the first trial.
  3. Counsel: Please identify that suggestion separately from the work of running the trial itself.
  4. Engineer: I have the dated meeting notes and the drawing showing the proposed inlet location.
  5. Counsel: Those records will help us understand the contribution without assigning inventorship from the job title.
  6. Engineer: We should also distinguish the suggestion from the final arrangement the team later adopted.
  7. Counsel: Exactly. Describe who proposed what and how the design developed, without rewriting the history.
  8. Engineer: I will preserve the original notes and explain the sequence in the confidential disclosure.
  9. Counsel: Then we can assess the relevant contributions against the claimed subject matter and applicable law.
  10. Engineer: I will not reduce Dana's role to testing merely because that is the title in the project plan.
An expected effect is not measured data

A patent professional reviews an invention summary.

  1. Engineer: The summary says the new channel reduces energy use, but that version has not been tested.
  2. Reviewer: Then describe the proposed mechanism and expected effect without presenting a measurement that does not exist.
  3. Engineer: We predict lower resistance because the path is smoother, although the actual result remains unknown.
  4. Reviewer: That explanation is useful when clearly separated from the data for the earlier prototype.
  5. Engineer: I can label the earlier graph with the configuration actually used in that test.
  6. Reviewer: Please do, and keep the later drawing from appearing to be the tested configuration.
  7. Engineer: Should the disclosure still mention the alternative even though we have no measurements for it?
  8. Reviewer: Describe the technical proposal accurately so the patent professional can assess the relevant disclosure questions.
  9. Engineer: I will remove the unsupported numerical claim and retain the factual design explanation.
  10. Reviewer: That improves accuracy without pretending the alternative was built, tested, or legally assessed already.
The conference slide is still a disclosure

Counsel discusses a proposed presentation.

  1. Researcher: I removed the patent title from the conference slide, so I assumed there is no disclosure concern.
  2. Counsel: The title is not the only issue; the technical content and circumstances need review before presentation.
  3. Researcher: The diagram still shows the channel arrangement, and the audience would not be under our internal confidentiality process.
  4. Counsel: Then send the actual slide and event details through the approved channel for prompt assessment.
  5. Researcher: We have not presented it or posted it online, and the event is next month.
  6. Counsel: Good. Preserve that status rather than reporting a disclosure as though it already occurred.
  7. Researcher: I should not assume that a general grace-period statement applies to every planned jurisdiction.
  8. Counsel: Correct. Real consequences depend on the actual facts and law, so do not rely on a universal shortcut.
  9. Researcher: I will hold the proposed technical slide for the required review and identify the responsible contact.
  10. Counsel: That lets the team address the issue before an intended presentation becomes an irreversible public event.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling
  1. Read the corrected interview and stress designed, built, suggested, and tested where their meanings differ.
  2. Switch roles and deliver the final document list using only the supplied development facts.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A drawing was mistaken for a test result

A second enclosure appears only in a dated sketch. It has not been built or tested. The team wants to include it in the confidential disclosure.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Engineer: "This is an alternative ___."

Answer and explanation

C. configuration

The sketch depicts another arrangement, not an established test outcome.

2. Reviewer: "Its performance remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. untested

The brief states that no construction or testing has occurred.

3. Engineer: "I will preserve the dated ___."

Answer and explanation

D. sketch

The dated drawing is the supplied development record for this version.

4. Reviewer: "Describe the expected effect as a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. hypothesis

An expected untested effect is a proposition to investigate, not a measured result.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Patent Law English
Professional audience: patent attorneys, patent agents, intellectual-property specialists, invention reviewers
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. Language practice, not patent or legal advice. Examples simplify documents and do not establish inventorship, patentability, validity, infringement, priority, filing deadlines, licensing rights, or freedom to operate. Real matters require qualified patent professionals, current law and office practice, and the actual documents. Protect confidential inventions and observe applicable filing, disclosure, security, and export requirements.
Lesson 01: Interviewing inventors without leading their answers
Original fictional case: Engineer Amira describes a prototype battery enclosure with a curved coolant channel. Technician Leo built it from drawings and suggested a detachable connector. One internal test reports a shorter cooling time, but the test conditions have not been compared. Patent professional Daniel is gathering facts, not deciding inventorship or patentability.
Language workshop: The improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling
Communication goal: Elicit the mechanism, alternatives, and individual contributions behind an invention disclosure.
Language guidance: Describe each contribution before assessing its legal significance. Building a prototype and conceiving a claimed feature are not interchangeable descriptions.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What does the curved channel change in the flow path? / Would the same effect occur with a straight channel? / Who first proposed that feature, and what records support the account? / The test reports a shorter time; it does not yet establish why. / Did Leo follow the drawings or propose a different arrangement? / We have not compared the test conditions yet. / Please use the approved confidential channel for the drawings. / We have a prototype, a reported result, and contribution details to verify. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: invention disclosure: Internal account of a proposed invention and its development. / technical problem: Practical technical difficulty the proposed invention addresses. / technical effect: Technical result attributed to a feature or process. / conception: Formation of the inventive idea under the applicable legal test. / reduction to practice: Implementation or legally relevant realization of an invention. / inventorship: Legal identification of the inventor or inventors. / contribution record: Account of who contributed what and when. / prototype: Preliminary implementation used to explore or test a design. / embodiment: Particular implementation described for an invention. / alternative configuration: Different arrangement of components or steps. / coolant channel: Passage through which cooling fluid moves. / detachable connector: Connection component designed to be separated. / causal mechanism: Process explaining how a feature produces a result. / comparative test: Test comparing implementations under stated conditions. / baseline configuration: Reference arrangement used for comparison. / laboratory notebook: Contemporaneous record of experimental work. / confidential disclosure: Communication within the relevant confidentiality arrangements. / public disclosure: Making information available outside the relevant confidential setting. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected interview and stress designed, built, suggested, and tested where their meanings differ. Switch roles and deliver the final document list using only the supplied development facts. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Amira: Engineer / Daniel: Patent professional
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A second enclosure appears only in a dated sketch. It has not been built or tested. The team wants to include it in the confidential disclosure.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Amira: Marketing wants to show the prototype at a public event next month. I have not approved that request.
Daniel: Flag the proposed public disclosure for prompt legal review before anyone assumes the timing is harmless.
Amira: I will supply the drawings, contribution details, test conditions, and event request as separate items for your review.
Daniel: That gives the invention disclosure a factual foundation without promising protection or treating an initial result as a verified product claim.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Vocabulary: invention disclosure: Internal account of a proposed invention and its development. / technical problem: Practical technical difficulty the proposed invention addresses. / technical effect: Technical result attributed to a feature or process. / conception: Formation of the inventive idea under the applicable legal test. / reduction to practice: Implementation or legally relevant realization of an invention. / inventorship: Legal identification of the inventor or inventors. / contribution record: Account of who contributed what and when. / prototype: Preliminary implementation used to explore or test a design. / embodiment: Particular implementation described for an invention. / alternative configuration: Different arrangement of components or steps. / coolant channel: Passage through which cooling fluid moves. / detachable connector: Connection component designed to be separated. / causal mechanism: Process explaining how a feature produces a result. / comparative test: Test comparing implementations under stated conditions. / baseline configuration: Reference arrangement used for comparison. / laboratory notebook: Contemporaneous record of experimental work. / confidential disclosure: Communication within the relevant confidentiality arrangements. / public disclosure: Making information available outside the relevant confidential setting. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.

Switch roles and deliver the final document list using only the supplied development facts. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Amira: Engineer / Daniel: Patent professional
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A second enclosure appears only in a dated sketch. It has not been built or tested. The team wants to include it in the confidential disclosure.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Amira: Marketing wants to show the prototype at a public event next month. I have not approved that request.
Daniel: Flag the proposed public disclosure for prompt legal review before anyone assumes the timing is harmless.
Amira: I will supply the drawings, contribution details, test conditions, and event request as separate items for your review.
Daniel: That gives the invention disclosure a factual foundation without promising protection or treating an initial result as a verified product claim.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Patent Law English
Professional audience: patent attorneys, patent agents, intellectual-property specialists, invention reviewers
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. Language practice, not patent or legal advice. Examples simplify documents and do not establish inventorship, patentability, validity, infringement, priority, filing deadlines, licensing rights, or freedom to operate. Real matters require qualified patent professionals, current law and office practice, and the actual documents. Protect confidential inventions and observe applicable filing, disclosure, security, and export requirements.
Lesson 01: Interviewing inventors without leading their answers
Original fictional case: Engineer Amira describes a prototype battery enclosure with a curved coolant channel. Technician Leo built it from drawings and suggested a detachable connector. One internal test reports a shorter cooling time, but the test conditions have not been compared. Patent professional Daniel is gathering facts, not deciding inventorship or patentability.
Language workshop: The improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling
Communication goal: Elicit the mechanism, alternatives, and individual contributions behind an invention disclosure.
Language guidance: Describe each contribution before assessing its legal significance. Building a prototype and conceiving a claimed feature are not interchangeable descriptions.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What does the curved channel change in the flow path? / Would the same effect occur with a straight channel? / Who first proposed that feature, and what records support the account? / The test reports a shorter time; it does not yet establish why. / Did Leo follow the drawings or propose a different arrangement? / We have not compared the test conditions yet. / Please use the approved confidential channel for the drawings. / We have a prototype, a reported result, and contribution details to verify. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.

02Discussing prior art without overstating the searchOne reference matches two features, not the entire claim+

Your goal

Explain a search result using feature mapping, dates, and the limits of the search scope.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

One reference matches two features, not the entire claim

Analyst Noor finds Reference R describing a housing and curved channel. The draft claim also requires a detachable connector, which Noor has not located in R. Reference S mentions a detachable connector in a different device. Dates and legal relevance need review. Counsel Ethan must distinguish the references and search limitations.

Noor: Search analyst / Ethan: Patent counsel

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which feature has not been located in R?

Answer and explanation

D. The detachable connector

Noor found the housing and channel but has not located the connector in R.

2. Where is a connector mentioned?

Answer and explanation

A. S, in a different device

The facts place the connector in S without establishing its presence in R.

3. What remains to be reviewed?

Answer and explanation

C. Dates and legal relevance

The case expressly leaves dates and legal significance unresolved for review.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Not located is a search finding, not proof of absence. A reference describing some features does not automatically disclose the whole combination.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
prior art
Earlier material relevant under the applicable patent law.assess prior art
novelty
Requirement that claimed subject matter be new under the applicable test.analyze novelty
inventive step
Requirement addressing whether an invention exceeds the applicable obviousness threshold.assess inventive step
nonobviousness
US patentability requirement concerning obviousness under applicable law.evaluate nonobviousness
reference document
Identified source considered in a patent analysis.retrieve the reference document
feature mapping
Comparison linking claimed features to disclosed material.prepare feature mapping
disclosure passage
Specific text or figure describing relevant subject matter.cite the disclosure passage
publication date
Date a document was published.verify the publication date
priority date
Claimed earlier date whose legal effect requires assessment.check the priority date
search scope
Boundaries of databases, terms, subject matter, and dates searched.define the search scope
classification code
Patent-search category identifying technical subject matter.search by classification code
keyword variant
Alternative term used to locate related material.include keyword variants
citation trail
Chain of references connected through citations.follow the citation trail
patent family
Related filings linked through specified priority relationships.review the patent family
non-patent literature
Relevant material outside patent documents.search non-patent literature
single-reference analysis
Examination of what one identified reference discloses.distinguish single-reference analysis
combination rationale
Reasoning for combining teachings under the applicable legal test.assess the combination rationale
search limitation
Constraint affecting search completeness or interpretation.disclose the search limitation
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Novelty and obviousness or inventive-step analyses have jurisdiction-specific tests. Do not combine references casually and call the result a single-reference disclosure.

C Notice the language

A useful finding can still be incomplete

Describe what a reference discloses before calling it decisive. Make uncertainty usable instead of hiding it behind a yes-or-no label.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate support
Which passage discloses the connector?
Qualify the finding
I have not located that feature in this reference.
Separate references
R describes the channel; S discusses a connector in another device.
Check dates
Publication and priority information still need verification.
Define search limits
The search covered these databases and keyword variants.
Avoid a guarantee
No close result in this search is not a guarantee of patentability.
Frame the legal issue
Any proposed combination needs the applicable legal analysis.
Report next steps
I will add pinpoint citations and document the remaining search gaps.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Not found versus absent
Not found reports a search result; absent makes a stronger statement about the material.
Discloses versus suggests
Discloses attributes material to a source; suggests often introduces an inference needing explanation.
Relevant versus decisive
Relevant warrants review; decisive claims that the material resolves the issue.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report sentence is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. R describes two features; its connector remains unlocated

The findings support a limited account of R, not a universal legal conclusion.

2. What makes feature mapping traceable?

Answer and explanation

B. Pinpoint passages or figures for each feature

Pinpoint support lets reviewers check each proposed mapping against the actual source.

3. What must an R-plus-S argument address?

Answer and explanation

D. Applicable combination analysis and its factual basis

A combination argument requires appropriate legal reasoning rather than automatic aggregation.

4. Which statement fairly describes an unsuccessful search?

Answer and explanation

B. No close result was located within the stated scope

A search result is bounded by coverage and does not establish universal patentability or clearance.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

One reference matches two features, not the entire claim

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Noor: Search analyst / Ethan: Patent counsel

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reference document
  • nonobviousness
  • search limitation
  • novelty
  • combination rationale
  • patent family
  • search scope
  • feature mapping
  • publication date
  • disclosure passage
  1. Noor
    Reference R looks close. It shows a housing and curved channel, which are both in our current draft claim.
  2. Ethan
    Start with 1 so we can see exactly which passages support each comparison.
  3. Noor
    The housing is in paragraph twelve and the channel in figure three, but I have not located the detachable connector.
  4. Ethan
    Preserve that 2. Not located in your review is more precise than declaring that the reference cannot disclose it.
  5. Noor
    Reference S mentions a detachable connector, although its device is different and the function is not described in the same way.
  6. Ethan
    Keep the 3 identities separate. We should not move a feature from S into the column describing R.
  7. Noor
    Could the two documents still raise a problem if neither appears to describe the entire draft combination alone?
  8. Ethan
    That calls for the applicable 4 or inventive-step analysis, including the facts and reasoning supporting any proposed combination.
  9. Noor
    I will describe the potential combination without saying that it automatically defeats the claim under every legal system.
  10. Ethan
    Identify a possible 5 for counsel to assess rather than treating shared subject matter as sufficient by itself.
  11. Noor
    The database gives several dates. I copied the earliest number without identifying what that date represents.
  12. Ethan
    Distinguish the 6 from claimed priority information, and verify the relevant legal timing separately.
  13. Noor
    Some results are related filings with different claims. I grouped them together to keep the report shorter.
  14. Ethan
    A 7 can organize results, but the actual document and claim version must remain identifiable.
  15. Noor
    We covered two databases, three classification groups, and synonyms for coolant routing, but not every technical publication.
  16. Ethan
    State that 8, including limits on non-patent literature and any language coverage.
  17. Noor
    The team wants the conclusion changed to no blocking prior art found, which sounds broader than the report supports.
  18. Ethan
    Keep the 9 discussion tied to the examined features; do not imply freedom to operate or a guarantee of grant.
  19. Noor
    I will revise the table, separate documents and dates, and flag the connector question for further review.
  20. Ethan
    Add the exact 10 for every positive mapping. That gives us a report we can analyze, challenge, and update.

Answers and explanations
  1. feature mapping Turn 2. Feature mapping links each claimed element to identified source material instead of equating overall similarity with complete disclosure.
  2. search limitation Turn 4. The search limitation states what has not been established and prevents an incomplete finding from becoming proof of absence.
  3. reference document Turn 6. Each reference document must remain separately identified so that a feature from S is not silently attributed to R.
  4. nonobviousness Turn 8. Nonobviousness concerns a distinct patentability analysis under US law, not automatic treatment of multiple documents as one disclosure.
  5. combination rationale Turn 10. A combination rationale supplies reasoning for considering teachings together and must be assessed under the relevant legal test.
  6. publication date Turn 12. The publication date identifies publication and must not be confused with other dates whose legal effect may differ.
  7. patent family Turn 14. A patent family groups related filings but does not establish identical claims or legal status for every member.
  8. search scope Turn 16. Search scope identifies actual coverage and makes the limits visible to readers interpreting the findings.
  9. novelty Turn 18. Novelty is a specific patentability question, so the search summary must not become a broad commercial-clearance conclusion.
  10. disclosure passage Turn 20. The disclosure passage allows direct verification of each proposed feature mapping rather than reliance on an unsupported summary.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Noor: Reference R looks close. It shows a housing and curved channel, which are both in our current draft claim.
  2. Ethan: Start with feature mapping so we can see exactly which passages support each comparison.
  3. Noor: The housing is in paragraph twelve and the channel in figure three, but I have not located the detachable connector.
  4. Ethan: Preserve that search limitation. Not located in your review is more precise than declaring that the reference cannot disclose it.
  5. Noor: Reference S mentions a detachable connector, although its device is different and the function is not described in the same way.
  6. Ethan: Keep the reference document identities separate. We should not move a feature from S into the column describing R.
  7. Noor: Could the two documents still raise a problem if neither appears to describe the entire draft combination alone?
  8. Ethan: That calls for the applicable nonobviousness or inventive-step analysis, including the facts and reasoning supporting any proposed combination.
  9. Noor: I will describe the potential combination without saying that it automatically defeats the claim under every legal system.
  10. Ethan: Identify a possible combination rationale for counsel to assess rather than treating shared subject matter as sufficient by itself.
  11. Noor: The database gives several dates. I copied the earliest number without identifying what that date represents.
  12. Ethan: Distinguish the publication date from claimed priority information, and verify the relevant legal timing separately.
  13. Noor: Some results are related filings with different claims. I grouped them together to keep the report shorter.
  14. Ethan: A patent family can organize results, but the actual document and claim version must remain identifiable.
  15. Noor: We covered two databases, three classification groups, and synonyms for coolant routing, but not every technical publication.
  16. Ethan: State that search scope, including limits on non-patent literature and any language coverage.
  17. Noor: The team wants the conclusion changed to no blocking prior art found, which sounds broader than the report supports.
  18. Ethan: Keep the novelty discussion tied to the examined features; do not imply freedom to operate or a guarantee of grant.
  19. Noor: I will revise the table, separate documents and dates, and flag the connector question for further review.
  20. Ethan: Add the exact disclosure passage for every positive mapping. That gives us a report we can analyze, challenge, and update.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The translation changed a search term

An analyst reviews multilingual search coverage.

  1. Analyst: The translated abstract calls the component a sleeve, but our original search used only the term housing.
  2. Counsel: That may explain a coverage gap; add relevant variants without assuming the words always describe identical structures.
  3. Analyst: I will compare the full document and figures before mapping the translated term to our feature.
  4. Counsel: Good. The translation can help locate material, but the actual technical disclosure still needs assessment.
  5. Analyst: The machine translation also changes the direction of flow in one sentence.
  6. Counsel: Flag that discrepancy for appropriate language review rather than relying on the fluent version alone.
  7. Analyst: I can preserve the source passage and the translation used during the search.
  8. Counsel: That makes the uncertainty traceable and prevents the report from hiding a material language issue.
  9. Analyst: The revised report will state which terms and language sources were covered.
  10. Counsel: Then readers can understand the search expansion without mistaking it for an unlimited completeness guarantee.
A later family member has different claims

A search reviewer checks grouped results.

  1. Reviewer: You grouped these documents as one family, but the later member appears to contain a different claim set.
  2. Analyst: I used the family grouping to reduce duplicate results, not to establish identical claim scope.
  3. Reviewer: Keep that purpose clear and identify the actual document supporting each relevant finding.
  4. Analyst: I will separate the publication numbers, dates, and claim versions in the comparison.
  5. Reviewer: We also need the current legal status where the review concerns rights affecting a product.
  6. Analyst: Yes. A family relationship alone does not say which claims are pending, granted, or no longer in force.
  7. Reviewer: The technical disclosure can still be relevant, but its legal use depends on the question.
  8. Analyst: I will distinguish a prior-art discussion from any separate freedom-to-operate assessment.
  9. Reviewer: That distinction will stop the commercial team from treating one consolidated row as complete clearance.
  10. Analyst: The report will retain the family link while preserving each material document's identity and status.
The search found nothing close in one database

An analyst reports a negative result.

  1. Manager: Your search found no close match. Can the presentation say that no relevant prior art exists?
  2. Analyst: No. It can say that no close result was located within the scope we actually searched.
  3. Manager: The current search used one database and only the English terms supplied by engineering.
  4. Analyst: Then both limits should stay visible rather than disappear from the executive summary.
  5. Manager: Could you identify the next coverage questions without promising a complete search of every possible source?
  6. Analyst: Yes. We can review relevant classifications, term variants, language coverage, and non-patent literature.
  7. Manager: I also should not describe the result as permission to launch the product.
  8. Analyst: Correct. That is a separate question involving the actual configuration, rights, territory, and review scope.
  9. Manager: I will keep the summary short but retain the phrase within the stated search scope.
  10. Analyst: That gives the team a useful finding without turning limited evidence into a universal assurance.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One reference matches two features, not the entire claim
  1. Read the corrected dialogue, stressing not located, separate, and within the stated scope.
  2. Switch roles and distinguish R alone from a proposed R-plus-S argument using the printed facts.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The abstract hides a useful figure

A reviewer finds a potentially relevant figure after the abstract search. The feature and legal date still need verification against the full document.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reviewer: "The abstract search did not identify this ___."

Answer and explanation

D. figure

The new finding is a figure, not a completed legal conclusion.

2. Counsel: "Check the complete ___ before mapping the feature."

Answer and explanation

A. disclosure

The mapping needs actual disclosed material rather than an abstract-only impression.

3. Reviewer: "The relevant date remains to be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. verified

The brief expressly leaves the relevant legal timing unresolved.

4. Counsel: "Update the report and preserve its stated ___."

Answer and explanation

B. scope

The new finding improves the report without creating an unlimited search guarantee.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.

Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
