03Explaining claim structure to a technical colleagueThe dependent claim adds a feature+

Your goal

Translate claim relationships into plain English without changing their scope.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

The dependent claim adds a feature

For this simplified drafting discussion, claim 1 requires a housing and channel. Claim 2 depends on claim 1 and adds a detachable connector. Engineer Priya thinks claim 2 covers a connector by itself. Patent professional Marcus explains the dependency and checks the draft term the valve, which has no clear earlier introduction. These are unfiled examples, not legal claim-construction conclusions.

Priya: Engineer / Marcus: Patent professional

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does claim 2 add in this example?

Answer and explanation

B. A detachable connector to the inherited requirements

The simplified dependency expressly carries forward claim 1 and adds the connector.

2. What is unclear about the valve wording?

Answer and explanation

D. Which earlier element the term identifies

The definite phrase has no clear earlier introduction identifying the intended element.

3. What is the document status?

Answer and explanation

C. An unfiled drafting example

The case explicitly describes unfiled examples rather than granted or adjudicated claims.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

In the supplied example, claim 2 contains the housing, channel, and connector requirements. It is not a claim to the connector alone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
claim
Statement defining the subject matter for which protection is sought or granted.review the claim
independent claim
Claim that does not depend on another claim.draft an independent claim
dependent claim
Claim incorporating a referenced claim and adding a further limitation.review a dependent claim
claim dependency
Relationship linking a dependent claim to another claim.check claim dependency
claim limitation
Requirement expressed in a claim.identify each claim limitation
claim scope
Extent of subject matter covered under the applicable interpretation.assess claim scope
antecedent basis
Earlier identification supporting a later reference to an element.check antecedent basis
preamble
Introductory portion of a claim.review the preamble
transitional phrase
Wording connecting claim introduction and body with legal significance.select the transitional phrase
claim body
Portion stating substantive elements or steps.analyze the claim body
apparatus claim
Claim directed to a device or system.distinguish an apparatus claim
method claim
Claim directed to steps of a process.draft a method claim
functional language
Wording describing what an element does.evaluate functional language
structural limitation
Requirement describing physical structure or arrangement.identify a structural limitation
further limitation
Additional restriction in a dependent claim.introduce a further limitation
claim construction
Legal interpretation of claim terms and scope.address claim construction
claim tree
Diagram or list showing claim dependencies.check the claim tree
fallback position
Narrower supported position available for appropriate consideration.preserve a fallback position
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

A plain-English explanation helps the discussion but does not replace the actual claim language or the applicable legal construction.

C Notice the language

Explain the relationship before the terminology

Technical colleagues may read claim lists as separate product options. Explain what carries forward before introducing drafting labels.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Explain inheritance
Claim 2 carries forward the requirements of claim 1.
Identify the addition
The connector is an added limitation, not a replacement.
Check the referent
Which valve does the later phrase refer to?
Locate the first mention
We need to check where that element is introduced.
Separate claim types
This version describes an apparatus rather than a sequence of steps.
Limit the paraphrase
That is a working explanation, not a legal construction.
Discuss narrowing
Adding a requirement narrows this simplified combination.
Keep alternatives distinct
A connector-only concept would need separate drafting and support review.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
In addition to versus instead of
In addition to adds a requirement; instead of substitutes one and changes the relationship.
A versus the
A can introduce an element; the ordinarily refers to an identified element. Exact drafting consequences require review.
Comprising versus consisting of
These can have materially different legal effects. Do not exchange them as ordinary stylistic synonyms.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which paraphrase matches the supplied claim 2?

Answer and explanation

A. A housing and channel with the added connector

The stated dependency retains claim 1 requirements and adds the connector.

2. Which question addresses antecedent basis?

Answer and explanation

A. Where is the valve first identified?

Antecedent basis concerns the earlier identification of the later referenced element.

3. What should a plain-language paraphrase avoid?

Answer and explanation

B. Silently dropping inherited limitations

Dropping inherited limitations would change the supplied combination and mislead the technical reader.

4. Which change requires substantive review rather than casual editing?

Answer and explanation

B. Replacing comprising with consisting of

Transitional phrases can affect legal scope and are not interchangeable stylistic choices.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

The dependent claim adds a feature

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Engineer / Marcus: Patent professional

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • claim limitation
  • claim tree
  • transitional phrase
  • claim dependency
  • dependent claim
  • claim construction
  • fallback position
  • antecedent basis
  • structural limitation
  • independent claim
  1. Priya
    Claim 2 says the enclosure of claim 1 has a detachable connector. Does that mean we now have protection for any connector?
  2. Marcus
    No. In this draft, the 1 includes the requirements it inherits from claim 1.
  3. Priya
    So the housing and channel remain part of the combination, even though their wording is not repeated on that line?
  4. Marcus
    Exactly. The 2 carries them forward, and the connector adds another requirement to that combination.
  5. Priya
    I had treated the numbered claims like a menu where the reader chooses one component and ignores the others.
  6. Marcus
    Think in terms of each 3. We must identify all the requirements in the particular claim under discussion.
  7. Priya
    Would a claim covering only the connector need to be drafted differently and checked against the original disclosure?
  8. Marcus
    Yes. A separate 4 would raise its own drafting and support questions; the current dependency does not accomplish that.
  9. Priya
    I also noticed the phrase the valve near the end, but I could not find a valve introduced earlier.
  10. Marcus
    That is an 5 issue to review. We need to establish which element the phrase is intended to identify.
  11. Priya
    The engineer meant a flow-control valve, not the detachable connector. I can point to the relevant drawing.
  12. Marcus
    Good. We should check the 6 and its support rather than guessing that two different component names mean the same thing.
  13. Priya
    Could I simplify comprising to consisting of? The second phrase sounds more natural to me in ordinary English.
  14. Marcus
    Do not change that 7 for style alone. The legal effect needs deliberate review under the applicable law.
  15. Priya
    I will leave the operative wording intact and add a separate plain-English explanation for the engineering meeting.
  16. Marcus
    That helps, provided you label it as a paraphrase rather than a definitive 8 that settles every possible interpretation.
  17. Priya
    I can show claim 1 at the top and claim 2 below it, with the connector marked as an addition.
  18. Marcus
    A 9 will make the relationship visible without dropping the inherited housing and channel requirements.
  19. Priya
    Then our summary is a housing and channel with a detachable connector, not a connector standing alone.
  20. Marcus
    Correct for this example. That narrower combination may be a 10 to assess, but it is not a promise of grant or an infringement conclusion.

Answers and explanations
  1. dependent claim Turn 2. A dependent claim retains the referenced requirements and adds a limitation rather than protecting the added element in isolation.
  2. claim dependency Turn 4. Claim dependency identifies the relationship that carries the earlier requirements into the dependent claim.
  3. claim limitation Turn 6. A claim limitation is a requirement of the claimed combination, not a menu item that can be omitted at will.
  4. independent claim Turn 8. An independent claim does not depend on another claim, but its drafting and legal support still require assessment.
  5. antecedent basis Turn 10. Antecedent basis concerns the earlier identification needed to make the later reference to the valve clear.
  6. structural limitation Turn 12. A structural limitation describes the physical element or arrangement, which must match the intended supported feature.
  7. transitional phrase Turn 14. A transitional phrase can affect legal scope, so these expressions must not be exchanged as stylistic synonyms.
  8. claim construction Turn 16. Claim construction is the legal interpretation of scope, not an informal engineering paraphrase.
  9. claim tree Turn 18. A claim tree displays dependencies and helps readers see which requirements are inherited by later claims.
  10. fallback position Turn 20. A fallback position is a narrower supported position for consideration, not a guarantee of patentability or protection.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: Claim 2 says the enclosure of claim 1 has a detachable connector. Does that mean we now have protection for any connector?
  2. Marcus: No. In this draft, the dependent claim includes the requirements it inherits from claim 1.
  3. Priya: So the housing and channel remain part of the combination, even though their wording is not repeated on that line?
  4. Marcus: Exactly. The claim dependency carries them forward, and the connector adds another requirement to that combination.
  5. Priya: I had treated the numbered claims like a menu where the reader chooses one component and ignores the others.
  6. Marcus: Think in terms of each claim limitation. We must identify all the requirements in the particular claim under discussion.
  7. Priya: Would a claim covering only the connector need to be drafted differently and checked against the original disclosure?
  8. Marcus: Yes. A separate independent claim would raise its own drafting and support questions; the current dependency does not accomplish that.
  9. Priya: I also noticed the phrase the valve near the end, but I could not find a valve introduced earlier.
  10. Marcus: That is an antecedent basis issue to review. We need to establish which element the phrase is intended to identify.
  11. Priya: The engineer meant a flow-control valve, not the detachable connector. I can point to the relevant drawing.
  12. Marcus: Good. We should check the structural limitation and its support rather than guessing that two different component names mean the same thing.
  13. Priya: Could I simplify comprising to consisting of? The second phrase sounds more natural to me in ordinary English.
  14. Marcus: Do not change that transitional phrase for style alone. The legal effect needs deliberate review under the applicable law.
  15. Priya: I will leave the operative wording intact and add a separate plain-English explanation for the engineering meeting.
  16. Marcus: That helps, provided you label it as a paraphrase rather than a definitive claim construction that settles every possible interpretation.
  17. Priya: I can show claim 1 at the top and claim 2 below it, with the connector marked as an addition.
  18. Marcus: A claim tree will make the relationship visible without dropping the inherited housing and channel requirements.
  19. Priya: Then our summary is a housing and channel with a detachable connector, not a connector standing alone.
  20. Marcus: Correct for this example. That narrower combination may be a fallback position to assess, but it is not a promise of grant or an infringement conclusion.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A method step became a component

A drafter checks an engineering paraphrase.

  1. Engineer: My summary lists the pump, housing, and cooling step together as though they were all components.
  2. Drafter: The cooling step describes an action, while the pump and housing describe physical elements.
  3. Engineer: We should separate the apparatus explanation from the sequence performed during operation.
  4. Drafter: Yes, and check the actual claim type instead of changing operative wording through the summary.
  5. Engineer: I can explain that fluid passes through the channel before returning to the reservoir.
  6. Drafter: That sequence is useful technical information, provided it matches the disclosed operation.
  7. Engineer: The diagram alone does not tell us whether the draft requires that exact order.
  8. Drafter: Correct. We need the actual wording and proper legal analysis for the claim-scope question.
  9. Engineer: I will label the sequence as an engineering explanation rather than a definitive claim construction.
  10. Drafter: Then the discussion stays clear without converting a teaching diagram into a legal conclusion.
The replacement word changed the relationship

Counsel reviews a proposed stylistic edit.

  1. Editor: I replaced connected to with adjacent to because the second phrase sounded less repetitive.
  2. Counsel: Those phrases describe different relationships; proximity does not necessarily establish a connection.
  3. Editor: The component can be near the housing without being attached in the way the drawing shows.
  4. Counsel: Exactly. A wording change needs technical and legal review, not just a judgment about style.
  5. Editor: I will restore the original wording in the working comparison and mark my suggestion separately.
  6. Counsel: Please preserve the change history so reviewers can see what was proposed and why.
  7. Editor: We should ask the engineer whether the connection is direct or through another component.
  8. Counsel: That can clarify the intended mechanism, while the patent professional reviews support and scope.
  9. Editor: I will not silently use a smoother synonym in the operative claim text.
  10. Counsel: That is the right distinction between improving readability and altering a substantive requirement.
A dependent claim cannot be read alone

A product manager asks about a claim chart.

  1. Manager: The chart shows the sensor only in claim 4. Does claim 4 cover any sensor we sell?
  2. Counsel: Not from that observation. We need to read its dependency and all inherited requirements.
  3. Manager: Claim 4 depends on claim 2, which already refers back to claim 1.
  4. Counsel: Then the claim tree matters; the sensor is not automatically protected in isolation.
  5. Manager: I can expand the chart to show which earlier limitations carry into the combination.
  6. Counsel: That will help the engineering team understand the structure without changing the actual claim language.
  7. Manager: Should we also identify the precise version, since the claim numbering changed during drafting?
  8. Counsel: Yes. Numbering alone is unreliable when different versions circulate in the same meeting.
  9. Manager: I will use the dated draft and label the chart as a working explanation.
  10. Counsel: Then everyone can discuss the same combination without mistaking an isolated feature for the entire claim.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The dependent claim adds a feature
  1. Read claim 1 and claim 2 aloud using the supplied paraphrases, emphasizing in addition to rather than instead of.
  2. Swap roles and explain the valve-reference problem without guessing which component the draft legally covers.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A different dependency changes the inherited features

Claim 3 depends on claim 2 and adds a pressure sensor. In this simplified example, claim 2 already includes the housing, channel, and connector.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Engineer: "Claim 3 inherits the earlier ___."

Answer and explanation

C. requirements

The stated dependency retains the requirements already included in claim 2.

2. Reviewer: "The sensor is an additional ___."

Answer and explanation

A. limitation

The new sensor adds a requirement to the inherited combination.

3. Engineer: "It does not replace the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. connector

The connector remains part of claim 2 and is inherited by claim 3.

4. Reviewer: "Show that relationship in the claim ___."

Answer and explanation

D. tree

A claim tree represents the dependency relationship described in the example.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Explaining claim structure to a technical colleague
Original fictional case: For this simplified drafting discussion, claim 1 requires a housing and channel. Claim 2 depends on claim 1 and adds a detachable connector. Engineer Priya thinks claim 2 covers a connector by itself. Patent professional Marcus explains the dependency and checks the draft term the valve, which has no clear earlier introduction. These are unfiled examples, not legal claim-construction conclusions.
Language workshop: The dependent claim adds a feature
Communication goal: Translate claim relationships into plain English without changing their scope.
Language guidance: In the supplied example, claim 2 contains the housing, channel, and connector requirements. It is not a claim to the connector alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Claim 2 carries forward the requirements of claim 1. / The connector is an added limitation, not a replacement. / Which valve does the later phrase refer to? / We need to check where that element is introduced. / This version describes an apparatus rather than a sequence of steps. / That is a working explanation, not a legal construction. / Adding a requirement narrows this simplified combination. / A connector-only concept would need separate drafting and support review. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: claim: Statement defining the subject matter for which protection is sought or granted. / independent claim: Claim that does not depend on another claim. / dependent claim: Claim incorporating a referenced claim and adding a further limitation. / claim dependency: Relationship linking a dependent claim to another claim. / claim limitation: Requirement expressed in a claim. / claim scope: Extent of subject matter covered under the applicable interpretation. / antecedent basis: Earlier identification supporting a later reference to an element. / preamble: Introductory portion of a claim. / transitional phrase: Wording connecting claim introduction and body with legal significance. / claim body: Portion stating substantive elements or steps. / apparatus claim: Claim directed to a device or system. / method claim: Claim directed to steps of a process. / functional language: Wording describing what an element does. / structural limitation: Requirement describing physical structure or arrangement. / further limitation: Additional restriction in a dependent claim. / claim construction: Legal interpretation of claim terms and scope. / claim tree: Diagram or list showing claim dependencies. / fallback position: Narrower supported position available for appropriate consideration. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read claim 1 and claim 2 aloud using the supplied paraphrases, emphasizing in addition to rather than instead of. Swap roles and explain the valve-reference problem without guessing which component the draft legally covers. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Priya: Engineer / Marcus: Patent professional
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Claim 3 depends on claim 2 and adds a pressure sensor. In this simplified example, claim 2 already includes the housing, channel, and connector.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: I can show claim 1 at the top and claim 2 below it, with the connector marked as an addition.
Marcus: A claim tree will make the relationship visible without dropping the inherited housing and channel requirements.
Priya: Then our summary is a housing and channel with a detachable connector, not a connector standing alone.
Marcus: Correct for this example. That narrower combination may be a fallback position to assess, but it is not a promise of grant or an infringement conclusion.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Checking drafting support before amendingThe new controller was developed after filing+

Your goal

Distinguish filed disclosure from later engineering developments when discussing an amendment.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

The new controller was developed after filing

The filed application describes a mechanical bypass. A later prototype uses an electronic controller. Engineer Sofia wants to insert the controller into the pending application as a clarification. Patent counsel Ravi has not located support for it in the filed text or drawings. The team must preserve both versions and obtain qualified advice on available filing options.

Sofia: Engineer / Ravi: Patent counsel

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which feature appears in the filed disclosure as described?

Answer and explanation

D. A mechanical bypass

The brief identifies a mechanical bypass as the feature described at filing.

2. What is Sofia proposing?

Answer and explanation

C. Adding the later controller as a clarification

She wants to insert the later controller while describing the change as clarification.

3. What has Ravi established?

Answer and explanation

D. He has not located support in the filed materials

The supplied finding is lack of located support, not a final legal or filing decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Calling a substantive addition a clarification does not supply support. Compare the proposed wording with the actual filed disclosure.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
specification
Written disclosure forming part of a patent application.review the specification
application as filed
Application content in its originally filed form.compare the application as filed
written description
Disclosure supporting the claimed subject matter under the applicable requirement.assess written description
enablement
Sufficiency of teaching under the applicable legal standard.evaluate enablement
new matter
Added subject matter not permitted by the applicable disclosure rules.identify potential new matter
amendment
Proposed or entered change to application documents.prepare an amendment
support citation
Pinpoint reference to material supporting proposed wording.provide a support citation
filed drawing
Drawing contained in the relevant filed application.inspect the filed drawing
later-developed feature
Feature developed after the earlier document or filing.distinguish a later-developed feature
original disclosure
Content disclosed in the original application.locate original disclosure
explicit support
Support stated directly in the relevant material.identify explicit support
inherent disclosure
Feature necessarily present under the applicable analysis rather than merely possible.assess inherent disclosure
draft history
Record of successive drafting versions.preserve the draft history
marked-up copy
Version visibly identifying proposed changes.circulate a marked-up copy
clean copy
Version showing text without visible change markings.compare the clean copy
support matrix
Table linking proposed wording to source disclosure.maintain a support matrix
priority entitlement
Right to rely on an earlier date under applicable requirements.assess priority entitlement
filing strategy
Plan for pursuing protection through appropriate filings.review the filing strategy
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Later development, earlier disclosure, and priority entitlement are separate questions. This scenario does not prescribe a filing route or deadline.

C Notice the language

Accuracy can slow an attractive shortcut

A later improvement may be valuable without belonging in the earlier filing. Explain the document boundary without dismissing the engineering work.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the basis
Where does the filed application disclose that controller?
Separate versions
That feature appears in the later prototype, not the cited filed passage.
Challenge a label
Calling it a clarification does not answer the support question.
Request precision
Please provide the exact paragraph or drawing reference.
Avoid hindsight
We should not read later knowledge back into the earlier document.
Preserve the record
Keep the filed version separate from the working draft.
Route the decision
We need advice on the available filing options.
Summarize the limit
The proposed addition remains under review; it is not approved for filing.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
As filed versus as amended
As filed identifies original content; as amended identifies content after a change.
Disclosed versus developed
A feature can have been developed without being disclosed in a particular application.
Necessarily versus possibly
Necessarily asserts an unavoidable feature; possibly states only a potential one.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What would most directly support the proposed wording?

Answer and explanation

A. A verified relevant passage or drawing in the filed disclosure

Support must be assessed against the relevant original disclosure, not supplied by a label.

2. Which summary avoids hindsight?

Answer and explanation

B. The controller is later developed and support is unlocated

The supplied facts distinguish later development from the unresolved original-support question.

3. Why keep a marked-up copy?

Answer and explanation

D. To make proposed wording changes visible for review

Visible changes help reviewers assess additions without altering the historical filed document.

4. Which action is appropriate now?

Answer and explanation

A. Review support and available filing options with counsel

Qualified review must resolve support and options before the team assumes a permissible filing step.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

The new controller was developed after filing

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sofia: Engineer / Ravi: Patent counsel

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • filing strategy
  • support citation
  • support matrix
  • later-developed feature
  • application as filed
  • new matter
  • written description
  • filed drawing
  • priority entitlement
  • marked-up copy
  1. Sofia
    The latest prototype uses an electronic controller. Can we add it to the pending application as a clarification of the bypass?
  2. Ravi
    First compare it with the 1. The version I have describes a mechanical bypass, not this later controller.
  3. Sofia
    The controller performs a related purpose, but we developed its arrangement after the filing and drew a new circuit diagram.
  4. Ravi
    Then identify it as a 2 while we investigate whether the proposed wording has support in the earlier disclosure.
  5. Sofia
    I found a broad sentence saying that flow may be controlled. Would that automatically support any electronic implementation?
  6. Ravi
    Not automatically. We need a proper 3 analysis rather than assuming a general result discloses every way of achieving it.
  7. Sofia
    I can show the exact sentence and the drawing it references, instead of sending only the revised paragraph.
  8. Ravi
    Please provide that 4 so we can evaluate the proposed wording against the actual text and figures.
  9. Sofia
    The new diagram is clearer. I was going to replace the old drawing in our shared folder to avoid confusion.
  10. Ravi
    Preserve the 5. Keep the new diagram separately labeled so that the historical record remains intact.
  11. Sofia
    I understand. Describing the addition as clarification does not establish that the original application taught this arrangement.
  12. Ravi
    Correct. We must assess potential 6 and the other disclosure requirements before deciding what can be changed.
  13. Sofia
    Would a table listing each proposed phrase beside its original source help you identify the unsupported parts?
  14. Ravi
    Yes. A 7 makes both positive citations and unresolved gaps visible without concealing them in a clean draft.
  15. Sofia
    I will also keep a version showing insertions and deletions, so the technical and legal reviewers see the same changes.
  16. Ravi
    Circulate the 8 alongside the clean version, with neither described as approved for filing.
  17. Sofia
    Management asked whether the electronic version would automatically receive the earlier date because it improves the same product.
  18. Ravi
    We cannot promise 9 on that basis. The relevant subject matter and actual disclosure need assessment.
  19. Sofia
    I will report that the later improvement is valuable, but the support and filing route remain unresolved.
  20. Ravi
    That is accurate. We can discuss 10 once we have the records, without inventing a deadline or assuming that every route is available.

Answers and explanations
  1. application as filed Turn 2. The application as filed is the historical disclosure against which the proposed addition must be assessed.
  2. later-developed feature Turn 4. A later-developed feature must remain distinguishable from what was actually disclosed in the earlier filing.
  3. written description Turn 6. Written description asks whether the relevant disclosure supports the claimed subject matter under the applicable legal standard.
  4. support citation Turn 8. A support citation points to the specific original material on which the proposed wording relies.
  5. filed drawing Turn 10. The filed drawing is part of the historical application record and must not be overwritten by a later engineering version.
  6. new matter Turn 12. New matter concerns impermissible added subject matter under applicable rules and cannot be avoided merely by calling an addition clarification.
  7. support matrix Turn 14. A support matrix links each proposed phrase to its claimed original basis and exposes missing or disputed support.
  8. marked-up copy Turn 16. A marked-up copy visibly identifies proposed changes so reviewers can assess their substance against the existing text.
  9. priority entitlement Turn 18. Priority entitlement depends on the applicable requirements and supported subject matter, not merely on improving the same product.
  10. filing strategy Turn 20. Filing strategy evaluates appropriate available routes after considering the actual disclosure, dates, jurisdictions, and legal requirements.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sofia: The latest prototype uses an electronic controller. Can we add it to the pending application as a clarification of the bypass?
  2. Ravi: First compare it with the application as filed. The version I have describes a mechanical bypass, not this later controller.
  3. Sofia: The controller performs a related purpose, but we developed its arrangement after the filing and drew a new circuit diagram.
  4. Ravi: Then identify it as a later-developed feature while we investigate whether the proposed wording has support in the earlier disclosure.
  5. Sofia: I found a broad sentence saying that flow may be controlled. Would that automatically support any electronic implementation?
  6. Ravi: Not automatically. We need a proper written description analysis rather than assuming a general result discloses every way of achieving it.
  7. Sofia: I can show the exact sentence and the drawing it references, instead of sending only the revised paragraph.
  8. Ravi: Please provide that support citation so we can evaluate the proposed wording against the actual text and figures.
  9. Sofia: The new diagram is clearer. I was going to replace the old drawing in our shared folder to avoid confusion.
  10. Ravi: Preserve the filed drawing. Keep the new diagram separately labeled so that the historical record remains intact.
  11. Sofia: I understand. Describing the addition as clarification does not establish that the original application taught this arrangement.
  12. Ravi: Correct. We must assess potential new matter and the other disclosure requirements before deciding what can be changed.
  13. Sofia: Would a table listing each proposed phrase beside its original source help you identify the unsupported parts?
  14. Ravi: Yes. A support matrix makes both positive citations and unresolved gaps visible without concealing them in a clean draft.
  15. Sofia: I will also keep a version showing insertions and deletions, so the technical and legal reviewers see the same changes.
  16. Ravi: Circulate the marked-up copy alongside the clean version, with neither described as approved for filing.
  17. Sofia: Management asked whether the electronic version would automatically receive the earlier date because it improves the same product.
  18. Ravi: We cannot promise priority entitlement on that basis. The relevant subject matter and actual disclosure need assessment.
  19. Sofia: I will report that the later improvement is valuable, but the support and filing route remain unresolved.
  20. Ravi: That is accurate. We can discuss filing strategy once we have the records, without inventing a deadline or assuming that every route is available.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The old draft was never filed

A reviewer checks a support citation.

  1. Engineer: I found the controller in an early draft, so I cited that paragraph as original filing support.
  2. Counsel: Was that wording actually included in the application as filed, or removed before submission?
  3. Engineer: It was removed during review. The filed copy describes only the mechanical arrangement.
  4. Counsel: Then the unfiled draft does not establish what the filed application disclosed.
  5. Engineer: I will preserve both versions and correct the support table to identify the actual filed text.
  6. Counsel: Good. We can then assess the real support question without treating drafting history as filed content.
  7. Engineer: The engineering team still wants protection for the controller developed later.
  8. Counsel: That request needs qualified advice on available options, not a retrospective change to the historical record.
  9. Engineer: I will mark the controller issue as unresolved and supply the development dates.
  10. Counsel: That gives the review the facts it needs without promising an earlier date or an available filing route.
The figure labels are inconsistent

A drafter checks an application drawing.

  1. Drafter: The text calls element twelve an inlet, but the figure legend calls the same number an outlet.
  2. Engineer: The drawing direction shows fluid entering there; the legend appears inconsistent with the intended design.
  3. Drafter: Please identify the source of that explanation and the relevant original materials.
  4. Engineer: I can provide the dated engineering drawing and explain the intended flow direction.
  5. Drafter: We still need to distinguish a proposed correction from a conclusion that the legal change is permissible.
  6. Engineer: Agreed. The patent professional should assess the actual filed record and available correction process.
  7. Drafter: I will mark both references rather than quietly replace every occurrence with inlet.
  8. Engineer: That will let reviewers see the discrepancy and the proposed resolution clearly.
  9. Drafter: The revised working copy will remain labeled as proposed until the review is complete.
  10. Engineer: Good. A clearer diagram should not conceal the fact that the filed text needs its own assessment.
Possible is not necessarily disclosed

Counsel asks about an asserted inherent feature.

  1. Engineer: The original device could use a wireless controller, so I assumed wireless control was inherently disclosed.
  2. Counsel: Could use describes a possibility; it does not establish that the feature was necessarily present.
  3. Engineer: The filed example can also work with a mechanical connection and no wireless component.
  4. Counsel: Then we cannot support your conclusion simply by saying the later wireless version is technically feasible.
  5. Engineer: I should explain the original mechanism and the alternative versions separately.
  6. Counsel: Yes. The applicable disclosure analysis needs the actual facts, not a stronger modal word inserted in summary.
  7. Engineer: I will change necessarily includes to could be implemented with in the technical note.
  8. Counsel: That is more accurate technically, but it still does not settle legal support for an amendment.
  9. Engineer: We will leave that conclusion to the patent review and preserve the original documents.
  10. Counsel: Exactly. Precise language helps the assessment without deciding it in advance.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The new controller was developed after filing
  1. Read the corrected exchange, stressing as filed, later developed, and proposed.
  2. Switch roles and state the three records to preserve: original documents, support matrix, and marked-up draft.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The new diagram is not the original diagram

A reviewer receives a clean drawing labeled revised. The original filed drawing and a change comparison have not been attached.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reviewer: "Please attach the original ___ drawing."

Answer and explanation

D. filed

The historical filed drawing is needed to compare the proposed changes.

2. Engineer: "I will also supply the change ___."

Answer and explanation

B. comparison

A change comparison makes the differences visible for review.

3. Reviewer: "The clean version alone does not establish ___."

Answer and explanation

B. support

A revised drawing does not itself prove support in the original disclosure.

4. Engineer: "The proposed revision remains under ___."

Answer and explanation

C. review

No approval or completed support assessment is supplied in the scenario.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

Lesson 04: Checking drafting support before amending
Original fictional case: The filed application describes a mechanical bypass. A later prototype uses an electronic controller. Engineer Sofia wants to insert the controller into the pending application as a clarification. Patent counsel Ravi has not located support for it in the filed text or drawings. The team must preserve both versions and obtain qualified advice on available filing options.
Language workshop: The new controller was developed after filing
Communication goal: Distinguish filed disclosure from later engineering developments when discussing an amendment.
Language guidance: Calling a substantive addition a clarification does not supply support. Compare the proposed wording with the actual filed disclosure.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Where does the filed application disclose that controller? / That feature appears in the later prototype, not the cited filed passage. / Calling it a clarification does not answer the support question. / Please provide the exact paragraph or drawing reference. / We should not read later knowledge back into the earlier document. / Keep the filed version separate from the working draft. / We need advice on the available filing options. / The proposed addition remains under review; it is not approved for filing. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: specification: Written disclosure forming part of a patent application. / application as filed: Application content in its originally filed form. / written description: Disclosure supporting the claimed subject matter under the applicable requirement. / enablement: Sufficiency of teaching under the applicable legal standard. / new matter: Added subject matter not permitted by the applicable disclosure rules. / amendment: Proposed or entered change to application documents. / support citation: Pinpoint reference to material supporting proposed wording. / filed drawing: Drawing contained in the relevant filed application. / later-developed feature: Feature developed after the earlier document or filing. / original disclosure: Content disclosed in the original application. / explicit support: Support stated directly in the relevant material. / inherent disclosure: Feature necessarily present under the applicable analysis rather than merely possible. / draft history: Record of successive drafting versions. / marked-up copy: Version visibly identifying proposed changes. / clean copy: Version showing text without visible change markings. / support matrix: Table linking proposed wording to source disclosure. / priority entitlement: Right to rely on an earlier date under applicable requirements. / filing strategy: Plan for pursuing protection through appropriate filings. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange, stressing as filed, later developed, and proposed. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Explaining an office action and agreeing on a responseThe examiner raised two different issues+

Your goal

Separate the examiner position, proposed response, and procedural status in a client update.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The examiner raised two different issues

A fictional US non-final office action rejects claim 1 over Reference R and separately questions the clarity of the term substantially aligned. The client wants to say the examiner rejected the product. Counsel Elise proposes reviewing the cited mapping and the unclear term before selecting a response. The actual notice controls all dates; no deadline or guaranteed outcome is supplied.

Jon: Client engineering lead / Elise: Patent counsel

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the notice address?

Answer and explanation

A. A prior-art rejection and a separate clarity issue

The case identifies a claim rejection and a separate concern about a term.

2. How should the examiner position be attributed?

Answer and explanation

C. The examiner states the cited mapping

The position comes from the office action and should not be turned into a different conclusion.

3. Which source controls the response timing?

Answer and explanation

B. The actual notice and applicable procedure

The brief supplies no date and directs timing to the actual notice and procedure.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The notice addresses claims and wording, not whether customers may buy the product. Examiner arguments, client disagreements, amendments, and allowance are separate statuses.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
office action
Official communication from a patent examiner or office.review the office action
non-final action
Procedural action not designated final in the relevant examination process.respond to a non-final action
ground of rejection
Stated legal basis for rejecting a claim.address each ground of rejection
examiner position
View or reasoning expressed by the examiner.summarize the examiner position
response deadline
Time limit governing the required response.verify the response deadline
docket entry
Recorded matter and deadline information in a tracking system.verify the docket entry
claim amendment
Proposed or entered change to claim wording.assess a claim amendment
argument on the record
Reasoning formally submitted in the examination record.develop an argument on the record
examiner interview
Discussion with an examiner under the applicable procedure.request an examiner interview
interview summary
Record of the substance and outcome of an examiner discussion.review the interview summary
allowance
Office determination permitting claims to proceed toward grant subject to requirements.distinguish allowance from issuance
final rejection
Procedural designation affecting available response routes.review a final rejection
appeal
Request for review under the applicable review procedure.assess an appeal
prosecution history
Record of proceedings during patent examination.review the prosecution history
clarity issue
Concern that wording does not identify its meaning sufficiently.resolve a clarity issue
distinguishing feature
Feature relied on to differentiate claimed subject matter from cited material.identify a distinguishing feature
response strategy
Plan for addressing the actual issues raised.agree on a response strategy
client instruction
Authorized direction from the client on the matter.obtain client instructions
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Non-final and final are procedural terms. They do not justify ignoring a notice or assuming that a particular response route, extension, or outcome is available.

C Notice the language

Challenge reasoning, not the person

A firm response can identify a disputed mapping without portraying the examiner as hostile or promising that disagreement will secure allowance.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Attribute the position
The examiner maps that passage to the channel limitation.
Separate issues
The clarity concern needs a response apart from the prior-art argument.
Disagree precisely
We do not read that passage as disclosing the stated relationship.
Discuss options
An amendment and an argument have different implications.
Avoid a promise
We can explain our position without guaranteeing allowance.
Confirm authority
Please confirm instructions after reviewing the proposed response.
Preserve timing
We will verify the actual deadline and docket entry.
Summarize status
The response is proposed; it has not yet been approved or filed.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Rejects versus prohibits
A claim rejection is not a general prohibition on making or selling a product.
Proposed versus entered
Proposed describes a requested change; entered indicates the relevant procedural acceptance into the record.
Final versus finished
Final is a procedural designation, not an ordinary-language guarantee that every possible step has ended.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which client update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. The office action raises prior-art and clarity issues for response

The notice concerns examination issues, not product prohibition, grant, or a promised result.

2. Which disagreement is professionally precise?

Answer and explanation

C. The cited passage does not appear to disclose that relationship

This statement challenges the reasoning with an identifiable issue rather than attacking the examiner.

3. What must happen before calling the response filed?

Answer and explanation

C. The actual filing must occur and be verified

Draft review and preference do not establish that a response has actually been filed.

4. Which summary keeps the issues separate?

Answer and explanation

B. Check the mapping and independently address the unclear term

Each stated issue needs appropriate attention rather than being erased by disagreement on another point.

E Conversations

The examiner raised two different issues

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jon: Client engineering lead / Elise: Patent counsel

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • ground of rejection
  • allowance
  • office action
  • response deadline
  • response strategy
  • prosecution history
  • distinguishing feature
  • claim amendment
  • examiner interview
  • client instruction
  1. Jon
    The examiner rejected our product. Management wants a quick assurance that your reply will reverse that decision.
  2. Elise
    The 1 addresses the claims and their wording, not whether the product can be sold or will succeed commercially.
  3. Jon
    The notice points to Reference R for claim 1 and also questions the phrase substantially aligned.
  4. Elise
    Those are separate issues. We need to address the 2 and examine the clarity concern rather than assume one answer resolves both.
  5. Jon
    Our engineer thinks the channel in R runs beside the housing instead of through the relationship described in our draft.
  6. Elise
    That may identify a 3. Please show the relevant passages and explain the technical relationship precisely.
  7. Jon
    We can provide a side-by-side diagram, but it should not introduce details that are missing from the filed application.
  8. Elise
    Correct. Any proposed 4 needs support and scope review, while our argument must stay consistent with the actual disclosure.
  9. Jon
    Could we first ask the examiner how that passage is being read instead of guessing the intended mapping?
  10. Elise
    We can assess an 5 under the applicable procedure. A discussion may clarify the issue without guaranteeing agreement.
  11. Jon
    If the conversation sounds encouraging, may I tell management that the application has been approved?
  12. Elise
    No. An encouraging discussion is not 6. We need an accurate record of what was discussed and the actual subsequent procedural status.
  13. Jon
    I will describe the response as proposed until we have reviewed the alternatives and authorized the next step.
  14. Elise
    That preserves 7. We should explain the consequences of an amendment and an argument before confirming the chosen response.
  15. Jon
    The notice is labeled non-final. Does that mean we can leave it until the next quarterly engineering meeting?
  16. Elise
    No. Verify the 8 and tracking record promptly; the label is not permission to disregard timing.
  17. Jon
    I also want the team to understand that a formal response becomes part of the examination record.
  18. Elise
    Yes. The 9 matters, so we should review technical explanations and legal arguments carefully before submitting them.
  19. Jon
    I will send the mapping and wording comments, then review your proposed alternatives with the authorized client contact.
  20. Elise
    We can then agree on the 10, keeping the notice, instructions, deadline, and eventual filing confirmation separate in the status report.

Answers and explanations
  1. office action Turn 2. The office action concerns patent examination issues and should not be misreported as a general rejection or prohibition of the product.
  2. ground of rejection Turn 4. The ground of rejection is the stated basis for rejecting the claim, distinct from a separate wording concern.
  3. distinguishing feature Turn 6. A distinguishing feature identifies the particular claimed difference to analyze, not a generalized assertion that the whole reference is irrelevant.
  4. claim amendment Turn 8. A claim amendment changes operative claim wording and requires support and scope review rather than being treated as an informal explanatory note.
  5. examiner interview Turn 10. An examiner interview can clarify issues under the applicable procedure but does not itself guarantee agreement or allowance.
  6. allowance Turn 12. Allowance is a formal examination status and must not be inferred from an encouraging informal discussion.
  7. client instruction Turn 14. Client instruction identifies the authorized direction needed for the matter and must not be assumed from informal interest in an option.
  8. response deadline Turn 16. The response deadline comes from the actual notice and procedure and cannot be postponed based on the word non-final.
  9. prosecution history Turn 18. The prosecution history preserves examination proceedings and submissions, so formal arguments require careful, consistent review.
  10. response strategy Turn 20. Response strategy coordinates the distinct issues, supported options, authority, and actual procedure without promising a favorable outcome.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jon: The examiner rejected our product. Management wants a quick assurance that your reply will reverse that decision.
  2. Elise: The office action addresses the claims and their wording, not whether the product can be sold or will succeed commercially.
  3. Jon: The notice points to Reference R for claim 1 and also questions the phrase substantially aligned.
  4. Elise: Those are separate issues. We need to address the ground of rejection and examine the clarity concern rather than assume one answer resolves both.
  5. Jon: Our engineer thinks the channel in R runs beside the housing instead of through the relationship described in our draft.
  6. Elise: That may identify a distinguishing feature. Please show the relevant passages and explain the technical relationship precisely.
  7. Jon: We can provide a side-by-side diagram, but it should not introduce details that are missing from the filed application.
  8. Elise: Correct. Any proposed claim amendment needs support and scope review, while our argument must stay consistent with the actual disclosure.
  9. Jon: Could we first ask the examiner how that passage is being read instead of guessing the intended mapping?
  10. Elise: We can assess an examiner interview under the applicable procedure. A discussion may clarify the issue without guaranteeing agreement.
  11. Jon: If the conversation sounds encouraging, may I tell management that the application has been approved?
  12. Elise: No. An encouraging discussion is not allowance. We need an accurate record of what was discussed and the actual subsequent procedural status.
  13. Jon: I will describe the response as proposed until we have reviewed the alternatives and authorized the next step.
  14. Elise: That preserves client instruction. We should explain the consequences of an amendment and an argument before confirming the chosen response.
  15. Jon: The notice is labeled non-final. Does that mean we can leave it until the next quarterly engineering meeting?
  16. Elise: No. Verify the response deadline and tracking record promptly; the label is not permission to disregard timing.
  17. Jon: I also want the team to understand that a formal response becomes part of the examination record.
  18. Elise: Yes. The prosecution history matters, so we should review technical explanations and legal arguments carefully before submitting them.
  19. Jon: I will send the mapping and wording comments, then review your proposed alternatives with the authorized client contact.
  20. Elise: We can then agree on the response strategy, keeping the notice, instructions, deadline, and eventual filing confirmation separate in the status report.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An interview note overstated agreement

A coordinator reviews a meeting summary.

  1. Coordinator: The examiner said the distinction was clearer after our explanation, so I wrote that the claim was allowed.
  2. Counsel: That overstates the discussion. Clarity during an interview is not a formal allowance.
  3. Coordinator: I should record what was discussed and whether any specific next step was identified.
  4. Counsel: Yes. Keep the actual interview outcome separate from the status of the application.
  5. Coordinator: The examiner asked us to submit the supported argument in the response for review.
  6. Counsel: Then report that request rather than claiming the later review has already been completed.
  7. Coordinator: I will revise the note and preserve the original meeting record for the responsible professional.
  8. Counsel: Also ensure the actual response requirements and deadlines remain properly tracked.
  9. Coordinator: The update will say the issue was discussed and a response remains to be submitted.
  10. Counsel: That gives the client a useful progress report without turning an encouraging exchange into a grant decision.
The technical disagreement needs a pinpoint

Counsel reviews an engineer's response comments.

  1. Engineer: My comment says the reference is completely wrong, but I have not identified the disputed passage.
  2. Counsel: Please show the specific mapping you disagree with and explain the technical difference.
  3. Engineer: The cited figure places the channel beside the enclosure, while our claim describes a different relationship.
  4. Counsel: That is a focused point we can assess against the actual wording and disclosure.
  5. Engineer: I will avoid attacking the examiner or implying that every part of the reference is irrelevant.
  6. Counsel: Good. A firm response should address the reasoning, not speculate about the person's motives.
  7. Engineer: We also need to keep the separate clarity issue visible in the response plan.
  8. Counsel: Exactly. Success on one argument would not automatically resolve every issue raised in the notice.
  9. Engineer: I will provide a marked comparison and the supporting technical explanation for your review.
  10. Counsel: Then we can evaluate a properly supported response without promising that the examiner will agree.
Final is a procedural label

A client asks about a later notice.

  1. Client: This notice says final rejection. Does that mean there is nothing left to discuss and no deadline to track?
  2. Counsel: No. The label has procedural consequences, and the actual options and timing need prompt review.
  3. Client: We should not treat it as the ordinary word finished and simply close the internal file.
  4. Counsel: Correct. Nor should we assume that any particular amendment, appeal, or extension is available without checking.
  5. Client: I can send the full notice and confirm the current authorized client contact.
  6. Counsel: That will help us explain the actual posture and obtain instructions on the appropriate options.
  7. Client: Management needs a concise account of what changed since the previous update.
  8. Counsel: We can describe the procedural status, issues, verified timing, and options requiring a decision.
  9. Client: I will avoid promising either automatic success or that every possible route is closed.
  10. Counsel: That preserves the distinction between a procedural designation and an unsupported conclusion about the entire matter.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The examiner raised two different issues
  1. Read the corrected update and distinguish the examiner position from the team response.
  2. Switch roles and deliver the final action sequence without supplying a deadline or promising allowance.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A draft reply was marked filed

The response draft has been approved internally, but no submission or office receipt has been verified.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The draft is internally ___."

Answer and explanation

D. approved

Internal approval is the completed step supplied in the scenario.

2. Counsel: "That does not establish that it was ___."

Answer and explanation

B. filed

Approval alone does not prove submission to the patent office.

3. Coordinator: "I will check the actual submission ___."

Answer and explanation

B. record

The actual filing record is needed to verify the event.

4. Counsel: "Keep the deadline ___ until its status is verified."

Answer and explanation

C. visible

Unverified filing requires continued attention to the real timing obligation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 05: Explaining an office action and agreeing on a response
Original fictional case: A fictional US non-final office action rejects claim 1 over Reference R and separately questions the clarity of the term substantially aligned. The client wants to say the examiner rejected the product. Counsel Elise proposes reviewing the cited mapping and the unclear term before selecting a response. The actual notice controls all dates; no deadline or guaranteed outcome is supplied.
Language workshop: The examiner raised two different issues
Communication goal: Separate the examiner position, proposed response, and procedural status in a client update.
Language guidance: The notice addresses claims and wording, not whether customers may buy the product. Examiner arguments, client disagreements, amendments, and allowance are separate statuses.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The examiner maps that passage to the channel limitation. / The clarity concern needs a response apart from the prior-art argument. / We do not read that passage as disclosing the stated relationship. / An amendment and an argument have different implications. / We can explain our position without guaranteeing allowance. / Please confirm instructions after reviewing the proposed response. / We will verify the actual deadline and docket entry. / The response is proposed; it has not yet been approved or filed. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: office action: Official communication from a patent examiner or office. / non-final action: Procedural action not designated final in the relevant examination process. / ground of rejection: Stated legal basis for rejecting a claim. / examiner position: View or reasoning expressed by the examiner. / response deadline: Time limit governing the required response. / docket entry: Recorded matter and deadline information in a tracking system. / claim amendment: Proposed or entered change to claim wording. / argument on the record: Reasoning formally submitted in the examination record. / examiner interview: Discussion with an examiner under the applicable procedure. / interview summary: Record of the substance and outcome of an examiner discussion. / allowance: Office determination permitting claims to proceed toward grant subject to requirements. / final rejection: Procedural designation affecting available response routes. / appeal: Request for review under the applicable review procedure. / prosecution history: Record of proceedings during patent examination. / clarity issue: Concern that wording does not identify its meaning sufficiently. / distinguishing feature: Feature relied on to differentiate claimed subject matter from cited material. / response strategy: Plan for addressing the actual issues raised. / client instruction: Authorized direction from the client on the matter. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.
