Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Vocabulary: office action: Official communication from a patent examiner or office. / non-final action: Procedural action not designated final in the relevant examination process. / ground of rejection: Stated legal basis for rejecting a claim. / examiner position: View or reasoning expressed by the examiner. / response deadline: Time limit governing the required response. / docket entry: Recorded matter and deadline information in a tracking system. / claim amendment: Proposed or entered change to claim wording. / argument on the record: Reasoning formally submitted in the examination record. / examiner interview: Discussion with an examiner under the applicable procedure. / interview summary: Record of the substance and outcome of an examiner discussion. / allowance: Office determination permitting claims to proceed toward grant subject to requirements. / final rejection: Procedural designation affecting available response routes. / appeal: Request for review under the applicable review procedure. / prosecution history: Record of proceedings during patent examination. / clarity issue: Concern that wording does not identify its meaning sufficiently. / distinguishing feature: Feature relied on to differentiate claimed subject matter from cited material. / response strategy: Plan for addressing the actual issues raised. / client instruction: Authorized direction from the client on the matter. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected update and distinguish the examiner position from the team response. Switch roles and deliver the final action sequence without supplying a deadline or promising allowance. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Jon: Client engineering lead / Elise: Patent counsel
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The response draft has been approved internally, but no submission or office receipt has been verified.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jon: I also want the team to understand that a formal response becomes part of the examination record.
Elise: Yes. The prosecution history matters, so we should review technical explanations and legal arguments carefully before submitting them.
Jon: I will send the mapping and wording comments, then review your proposed alternatives with the authorized client contact.
Elise: We can then agree on the response strategy, keeping the notice, instructions, deadline, and eventual filing confirmation separate in the status report.

06Coordinating international patent filing decisionsAn international report is not a worldwide grant+

Your goal

Explain the PCT process and obtain specific instructions without promising universal protection.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

An international report is not a worldwide grant

Coordinator Hana has received an international search report and written opinion for a PCT application. Manager Luis calls it a world patent. The company is evaluating markets A, B, and C but has authorized none for the national or regional stage. Local requirements, costs, translations, and actual deadlines remain for verification with the responsible professionals.

Luis: IP portfolio manager / Hana: Filing coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What documents have arrived?

Answer and explanation

A. An international search report and written opinion

The case supplies international-stage reports, not national or worldwide grants.

2. Which markets are authorized for the next stage?

Answer and explanation

C. None has yet been authorized

The brief expressly says no market has been authorized for national or regional action.

3. Which matters need verification?

Answer and explanation

B. Requirements, costs, translations, and actual deadlines

These implementation and timing matters remain unresolved in the supplied case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A PCT application is not a world patent. International-stage reports inform further decisions; national or regional offices determine grant under their applicable systems.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
Patent Cooperation Treaty
Treaty providing an international patent application procedure.use the Patent Cooperation Treaty route
international application
Application filed under the relevant international procedure.identify the international application
receiving Office
Office receiving a PCT application under the applicable rules.identify the receiving Office
International Searching Authority
Authority conducting the relevant PCT international search.identify the International Searching Authority
international search report
Report identifying relevant search results in the PCT process.review the international search report
written opinion
Written international-stage assessment under the relevant PCT procedure.review the written opinion
international phase
International stage of the PCT application process.complete international-phase tasks
national phase
Stage before national offices under the applicable PCT framework.plan national-phase entry
regional phase
Stage before a regional office under the applicable framework.coordinate regional-phase entry
designated Office
Office associated with a designation under the PCT procedure.verify designated Office requirements
national requirements
Requirements applicable before the relevant national office.verify national requirements
translation specification
Requirements for the language version needed in a filing.confirm the translation specification
filing instruction
Authorized direction identifying a requested filing action.obtain filing instructions
jurisdiction budget
Planned expenditure for pursuing protection in a territory.approve the jurisdiction budget
local associate
Local patent professional assisting with a jurisdictional matter.instruct the local associate
grant decision
Decision by the competent office on granting patent rights.distinguish the grant decision
territorial protection
Rights limited to the relevant territory and legal scope.explain territorial protection
portfolio review
Assessment of filings and rights against organizational priorities.conduct a portfolio review
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

This exercise provides no filing deadline or route-specific instruction. Actual dates, offices, translations, fees, and procedures require current verification.

C Notice the language

Translate the process into decision points

A manager needs to know what has happened, what still requires a decision, and who is responsible. Avoid turning a process diagram into a promise of grant.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Correct the label
We have a PCT application, not a worldwide grant.
Explain the report
The written opinion is an assessment, not a patent certificate.
Identify the decision
Which markets are authorized for the next stage?
Separate interest from instruction
Evaluating a country is not an instruction to file there.
Confirm responsibilities
Who verifies the local requirements and deadline?
Clarify costs
Does this estimate include translation and local professional fees?
Preserve territorial scope
Any resulting rights must be identified by territory and status.
Close the loop
We need explicit instructions and confirmation of the action taken.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Application versus patent
An application seeks rights; a granted patent has the actual status and scope established by the relevant system.
Designated versus entered
Designation in a treaty procedure and completion of national-stage actions are different concepts.
Estimate versus authorization
An estimate informs a decision; it does not authorize expenditure or filing.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which description corrects world patent accurately?

Answer and explanation

C. A PCT application with international-stage reports

The supplied process is an application route, not universal grant or commercial permission.

2. What converts market interest into a filing instruction?

Answer and explanation

A. Explicit authorized direction for the specified action

A specific authorized instruction is distinct from exploratory discussion and cost information.

3. What should a budget comparison identify?

Answer and explanation

B. What each estimate includes and excludes

Comparable budgets require clear coverage of local fees, translation, and other stated items.

4. Which status remains unresolved?

Answer and explanation

A. National or regional action for the evaluated markets

No market authorization or completed next-stage action is supplied in the brief.

E Conversations

An international report is not a worldwide grant

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Luis: IP portfolio manager / Hana: Filing coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • portfolio review
  • international application
  • written opinion
  • territorial protection
  • grant decision
  • national requirements
  • translation specification
  • local associate
  • jurisdiction budget
  • filing instruction
  1. Luis
    The search report has arrived. Can I tell the board that our world patent now covers markets A, B, and C?
  2. Hana
    Please call it an 1 under the PCT. We do not have a worldwide grant from receiving those reports.
  3. Luis
    I see an international search report and a separate assessment discussing the claims. Do they perform the same function?
  4. Hana
    The 2 gives an assessment under that procedure; neither document itself grants patents in the markets on our list.
  5. Luis
    The reports will still help us decide whether the commercial opportunity justifies further work in each market.
  6. Hana
    Yes. They inform the 3, alongside business priorities and qualified advice on the actual findings.
  7. Luis
    The market slide lists A, B, and C. I assumed that putting them on the slide authorized your next steps.
  8. Hana
    We need a specific 4. Evaluation and authorization are different statuses, especially where costs and timing are involved.
  9. Luis
    Should the board expect exactly the same requirements in each place once the international stage is complete?
  10. Hana
    No. The relevant 5 or regional requirements must be checked with the responsible professionals using current information.
  11. Luis
    I have three estimates, but one includes translation and another mentions only the local professional fee.
  12. Hana
    Then the 6 comparison needs clearer assumptions before anyone treats those totals as equivalent.
  13. Luis
    We also need to know who checks the language and format of each version, not just who places the order.
  14. Hana
    Confirm the 7 and review responsibility. A completed translation is not proof that every filing requirement has been met.
  15. Luis
    The local firms can confirm the requirements and actual dates, but the central team must send consistent instructions.
  16. Hana
    Each 8 should receive the authorized scope, documents, and a request for confirmation of the action taken.
  17. Luis
    Once the national-stage actions are completed, should the status report say protection is granted in each place?
  18. Hana
    No. Entry and the 9 are separate. Report each application or right using its actual territory and procedural status.
  19. Luis
    I will revise the board slide to show reports received, market decisions pending, and local requirements under review.
  20. Hana
    Good. That explains 10 without promising grants, inventing deadlines, or treating the application as permission to commercialize everywhere.

Answers and explanations
  1. international application Turn 2. An international application under the PCT is a filing procedure, not a worldwide granted patent.
  2. written opinion Turn 4. The written opinion provides an international-stage assessment and must not be presented as a certificate granting patent rights.
  3. portfolio review Turn 6. Portfolio review compares the filings and available information with business priorities without treating an assessment as a grant.
  4. filing instruction Turn 8. A filing instruction is an authorized direction for a specified action and cannot be inferred from a list of markets under evaluation.
  5. national requirements Turn 10. National requirements are office-specific and need verification rather than being assumed identical across markets.
  6. jurisdiction budget Turn 12. A jurisdiction budget must identify included and excluded costs so that figures for different territories can be compared meaningfully.
  7. translation specification Turn 14. The translation specification identifies the actual language and filing requirements that the prepared version must satisfy.
  8. local associate Turn 16. The local associate provides the relevant local professional coordination, while the central team must preserve clear instructions and accountability.
  9. grant decision Turn 18. The grant decision belongs to the competent office and remains distinct from completing national or regional entry actions.
  10. territorial protection Turn 20. Territorial protection requires identifying where rights exist and their actual scope and status, rather than implying universal coverage.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Luis: The search report has arrived. Can I tell the board that our world patent now covers markets A, B, and C?
  2. Hana: Please call it an international application under the PCT. We do not have a worldwide grant from receiving those reports.
  3. Luis: I see an international search report and a separate assessment discussing the claims. Do they perform the same function?
  4. Hana: The written opinion gives an assessment under that procedure; neither document itself grants patents in the markets on our list.
  5. Luis: The reports will still help us decide whether the commercial opportunity justifies further work in each market.
  6. Hana: Yes. They inform the portfolio review, alongside business priorities and qualified advice on the actual findings.
  7. Luis: The market slide lists A, B, and C. I assumed that putting them on the slide authorized your next steps.
  8. Hana: We need a specific filing instruction. Evaluation and authorization are different statuses, especially where costs and timing are involved.
  9. Luis: Should the board expect exactly the same requirements in each place once the international stage is complete?
  10. Hana: No. The relevant national requirements or regional requirements must be checked with the responsible professionals using current information.
  11. Luis: I have three estimates, but one includes translation and another mentions only the local professional fee.
  12. Hana: Then the jurisdiction budget comparison needs clearer assumptions before anyone treats those totals as equivalent.
  13. Luis: We also need to know who checks the language and format of each version, not just who places the order.
  14. Hana: Confirm the translation specification and review responsibility. A completed translation is not proof that every filing requirement has been met.
  15. Luis: The local firms can confirm the requirements and actual dates, but the central team must send consistent instructions.
  16. Hana: Each local associate should receive the authorized scope, documents, and a request for confirmation of the action taken.
  17. Luis: Once the national-stage actions are completed, should the status report say protection is granted in each place?
  18. Hana: No. Entry and the grant decision are separate. Report each application or right using its actual territory and procedural status.
  19. Luis: I will revise the board slide to show reports received, market decisions pending, and local requirements under review.
  20. Hana: Good. That explains territorial protection without promising grants, inventing deadlines, or treating the application as permission to commercialize everywhere.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The estimate excludes translation review

A coordinator compares local filing estimates.

  1. Manager: The lower estimate looks attractive, but its total does not mention translation review or local filing fees.
  2. Coordinator: Then it is not directly comparable with the estimate that includes those items.
  3. Manager: I will ask each provider to identify inclusions, exclusions, currency, and the assumptions behind the amount.
  4. Coordinator: Good. We also need to distinguish an estimate from authority to incur the expense.
  5. Manager: The client has not yet approved the market list or the budget.
  6. Coordinator: Then the status should remain evaluation pending rather than filing instructed.
  7. Manager: We can collect the information now while the responsible professionals verify actual timing.
  8. Coordinator: Yes. Budget uncertainty should not hide a real deadline that needs prompt attention.
  9. Manager: The comparison will show equivalent categories instead of ranking unrelated headline totals.
  10. Coordinator: That will support a clearer decision without suggesting that a lower estimate guarantees the same service or outcome.
The written opinion sounds favorable

Counsel explains an international-stage report.

  1. Manager: The written opinion is encouraging. May we call it a successful patent grant in the press summary?
  2. Counsel: No. It is an international-stage assessment, not a grant from every office we may approach.
  3. Manager: We can describe receipt of the report and the next portfolio decisions instead.
  4. Counsel: Yes, with suitable review of any public statement and the actual scope of the findings.
  5. Manager: The report does not decide whether we should enter every possible market.
  6. Counsel: Correct. Business priorities, costs, local advice, and the actual findings all matter to that decision.
  7. Manager: I will keep national or regional grant status separate from the report's conclusions.
  8. Counsel: That prevents a favorable assessment from becoming a claim of territorial rights that do not yet exist.
  9. Manager: The board update will identify which decisions are authorized and which remain under evaluation.
  10. Counsel: Good. Clear status language is more useful than a headline that collapses the entire application process.
The instruction names the wrong applicant

A coordinator verifies filing particulars.

  1. Associate: Your instruction names the group brand, but the application documents identify a specific legal entity.
  2. Coordinator: Thank you for flagging it. We should verify the actual applicant details before treating the names as equivalent.
  3. Associate: The local forms also need consistent information rather than an informal commercial abbreviation.
  4. Coordinator: I will check the approved records and obtain the necessary clarification through the responsible professional.
  5. Associate: Please preserve the existing document version while that verification takes place.
  6. Coordinator: Agreed. I will not silently substitute an entity merely because the brand is familiar.
  7. Associate: We also need to keep the real filing timetable visible during the correction.
  8. Coordinator: Yes. I will route the discrepancy promptly with the exact documents and unresolved question.
  9. Associate: The revised instruction should clearly identify what has been verified and by whom.
  10. Coordinator: Then the local action can proceed on accurate authorized particulars rather than an assumption about corporate identity.

Vocabulary: Patent Cooperation Treaty: Treaty providing an international patent application procedure. / international application: Application filed under the relevant international procedure. / receiving Office: Office receiving a PCT application under the applicable rules. / International Searching Authority: Authority conducting the relevant PCT international search. / international search report: Report identifying relevant search results in the PCT process. / written opinion: Written international-stage assessment under the relevant PCT procedure. / international phase: International stage of the PCT application process. / national phase: Stage before national offices under the applicable PCT framework. / regional phase: Stage before a regional office under the applicable framework. / designated Office: Office associated with a designation under the PCT procedure. / national requirements: Requirements applicable before the relevant national office. / translation specification: Requirements for the language version needed in a filing. / filing instruction: Authorized direction identifying a requested filing action. / jurisdiction budget: Planned expenditure for pursuing protection in a territory. / local associate: Local patent professional assisting with a jurisdictional matter. / grant decision: Decision by the competent office on granting patent rights. / territorial protection: Rights limited to the relevant territory and legal scope. / portfolio review: Assessment of filings and rights against organizational priorities. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation and distinguish application, report, entry, and grant. Switch roles and state what the three estimates need before the board can compare them. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Luis: IP portfolio manager / Hana: Filing coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A local associate sends an estimate for market B. The client has not authorized filing or approved the budget.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: Once the national-stage actions are completed, should the status report say protection is granted in each place?
Hana: No. Entry and the grant decision are separate. Report each application or right using its actual territory and procedural status.
Luis: I will revise the board slide to show reports received, market decisions pending, and local requirements under review.
Hana: Good. That explains territorial protection without promising grants, inventing deadlines, or treating the application as permission to commercialize everywhere.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

07Explaining freedom to operate and design-around questionsOur own patent does not answer the launch question+

Your goal

Clarify the product, territory, claim status, and scope of a commercial-clearance review.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Our own patent does not answer the launch question

Product lead Mei says the company can launch its enclosure because it owns a patent. Counsel Arun explains that a separate freedom-to-operate review concerns third-party rights and the actual product configuration in market A. The engineers propose moving a connector but have not supplied revised drawings. No clearance opinion, infringement finding, or design-around conclusion has been issued.

Mei: Product lead / Arun: Patent counsel

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why does ownership not settle the launch question?

Answer and explanation

B. Third-party rights may require separate review

The brief distinguishes the company patent from possible third-party rights affecting the actual product.

2. What is missing for the proposed connector change?

Answer and explanation

A. Revised drawings

The engineers propose a change but have not provided the revised configuration.

3. Which conclusion has been issued?

Answer and explanation

D. None of the stated legal conclusions

The brief expressly says that no such opinion, finding, or conclusion has been issued.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A patent confers exclusion rights within its scope; it is not a universal permission to commercialize. Patentability and freedom to operate ask different questions.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
freedom to operate
Assessment of relevant rights affecting specified commercial activity in a territory.commission a freedom-to-operate review
right to exclude
Patent right to prevent specified acts within its legal scope.explain the right to exclude
third-party patent
Patent belonging to another party.review third-party patents
commercial clearance
Qualified assessment addressing specified launch or commercial risks.define commercial-clearance scope
product configuration
Particular design and components being evaluated.freeze the reviewed product configuration
territory of interest
Geographic jurisdiction relevant to the proposed activity.identify the territory of interest
legal status
Current procedural or rights status of a patent or application.verify legal status
claim chart
Structured comparison of claim requirements with relevant features.prepare a claim chart
literal infringement
Infringement analysis based on the claim requirements under applicable interpretation.assess literal infringement
doctrine of equivalents
Jurisdiction-specific doctrine addressing certain equivalents beyond literal wording.review the doctrine of equivalents
design-around
Product change intended to avoid relevant patent scope, requiring assessment.evaluate a design-around
blocking patent
Patent whose relevant scope may constrain proposed commercial activity.investigate a potential blocking patent
expired patent
Patent whose term or rights have ended subject to actual legal status.verify an expired patent
pending application
Application not yet resolved by the relevant final status.monitor a pending application
continuing application
Related further application under an applicable filing system.review continuing applications
clearance opinion
Professional opinion within a defined commercial-clearance scope.obtain a clearance opinion
residual risk
Risk remaining after the stated review or action.communicate residual risk
review trigger
Change or event requiring reassessment.define a review trigger
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Moving one feature does not automatically establish a successful design-around. The actual configuration, claims, status, territory, and applicable law need review.

C Notice the language

Separate encouragement from clearance

Acknowledge the value of the company patent while explaining why it does not settle rights held by others. Avoid letting launch pressure broaden an uncompleted review.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate ownership from permission
Our own patent does not settle third-party rights.
Define the review
Which product version and market does the assessment cover?
Request the configuration
Please provide the revised drawings before we assess the change.
Avoid a shortcut
A different appearance does not by itself establish a design-around.
Check the record
We need the current claims and legal status.
Qualify the conclusion
Any opinion must state its scope, assumptions, and limits.
Control changes
A material design change should trigger reassessment.
Report accurately
Clearance is under review, not already confirmed.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Own versus may use
Own identifies a property relationship; may use can imply permission that requires separate analysis.
Looks different versus falls outside
Visual difference and legal scope are not equivalent tests.
For this version versus for every version
The first limits the assessment; the second expands it beyond the reviewed configuration.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which launch statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. Third-party-rights review remains incomplete

The case provides no completed clearance or design-around assessment.

2. What is necessary to assess the proposed change?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual revised configuration and relevant claims

Assessment requires the changed design and relevant legal material rather than appearance or ownership alone.

3. Which scope statement is appropriately bounded?

Answer and explanation

B. This product configuration in market A

The brief identifies a specific configuration and market, not universal future clearance.

4. Which event should be flagged for reassessment?

Answer and explanation

B. A material change to the reviewed product

A material product change can affect the basis of the actual review.

E Conversations

Our own patent does not answer the launch question

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Product lead / Arun: Patent counsel

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • claim chart
  • territory of interest
  • product configuration
  • clearance opinion
  • residual risk
  • right to exclude
  • freedom to operate
  • legal status
  • design-around
  • review trigger
  1. Mei
    We own a patent on the enclosure, so I told the launch team that the product is clear to sell in market A.
  2. Arun
    Ownership gives us a 1 within the patent scope. It does not settle whether third-party rights affect our product.
  3. Mei
    Then the patentability search we commissioned earlier answered a different question from the one the launch team is asking now.
  4. Arun
    Correct. A 2 review needs a defined product, activity, territory, and current legal information.
  5. Mei
    Our first market is A, and the production design has changed since the version used in the earlier review.
  6. Arun
    Please identify the current 3 rather than assuming that an earlier drawing still represents what will be sold.
  7. Mei
    Engineering proposes moving the connector to the opposite side. The team thinks that should avoid the competitor patent.
  8. Arun
    We need the revised drawings before assessing that 4. A different appearance does not by itself answer the claim-scope question.
  9. Mei
    I can obtain a controlled drawing and a list of changed components, with the old version retained for comparison.
  10. Arun
    Good. We can use a 5 to organize the relevant comparison, subject to proper claim interpretation and legal analysis.
  11. Mei
    The search result includes a granted patent and a related application. Can we treat both as the same right?
  12. Arun
    No. Verify the 6 and actual claims for each relevant record. Related filings can differ in scope and procedural position.
  13. Mei
    We should also make clear that the report is about market A, not every country in the sales forecast.
  14. Arun
    Exactly. The 7 matters, along with the specified activities and the applicable law.
  15. Mei
    Management would like a short answer before the launch meeting, even if several factual questions remain unresolved.
  16. Arun
    We can give a concise status, but not label it a completed 8 while the configuration and relevant rights are still under review.
  17. Mei
    I will replace clear to sell with review ongoing and identify the missing drawing as the engineering team's next action.
  18. Arun
    That is accurate. The eventual assessment should also explain any 9 within its stated scope rather than promising that no claim can ever arise.
  19. Mei
    Once the design is reviewed, I will flag any further connector or channel change before relying on the same conclusion.
  20. Arun
    Make that a 10. A scoped assessment stays useful only when the team knows when its underlying facts have changed.

Answers and explanations
  1. right to exclude Turn 2. The right to exclude concerns specified acts within patent scope and does not itself authorize commercialization free of other rights.
  2. freedom to operate Turn 4. Freedom to operate concerns relevant rights affecting specified commercial activity, distinct from whether the company invention may be patentable.
  3. product configuration Turn 6. The product configuration is the actual design being assessed, so an earlier version cannot silently stand in for the changed production design.
  4. design-around Turn 8. A design-around is an intended avoidance change whose legal effectiveness requires assessment rather than being established by intent or visual difference.
  5. claim chart Turn 10. A claim chart compares relevant claim requirements with the product features and helps expose where the actual scope questions arise.
  6. legal status Turn 12. Legal status distinguishes granted rights, pending applications, and other relevant conditions rather than equating related documents.
  7. territory of interest Turn 14. The territory of interest bounds the jurisdictional review and cannot be expanded merely because another country appears in a sales forecast.
  8. clearance opinion Turn 16. A clearance opinion is a professional assessment within a defined scope, not a substitute label for an unfinished status update.
  9. residual risk Turn 18. Residual risk identifies what remains after the stated review and should not be hidden behind an absolute assurance.
  10. review trigger Turn 20. A review trigger is a change requiring reassessment because it may alter the factual basis of the previous conclusion.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mei: We own a patent on the enclosure, so I told the launch team that the product is clear to sell in market A.
  2. Arun: Ownership gives us a right to exclude within the patent scope. It does not settle whether third-party rights affect our product.
  3. Mei: Then the patentability search we commissioned earlier answered a different question from the one the launch team is asking now.
  4. Arun: Correct. A freedom to operate review needs a defined product, activity, territory, and current legal information.
  5. Mei: Our first market is A, and the production design has changed since the version used in the earlier review.
  6. Arun: Please identify the current product configuration rather than assuming that an earlier drawing still represents what will be sold.
  7. Mei: Engineering proposes moving the connector to the opposite side. The team thinks that should avoid the competitor patent.
  8. Arun: We need the revised drawings before assessing that design-around. A different appearance does not by itself answer the claim-scope question.
  9. Mei: I can obtain a controlled drawing and a list of changed components, with the old version retained for comparison.
  10. Arun: Good. We can use a claim chart to organize the relevant comparison, subject to proper claim interpretation and legal analysis.
  11. Mei: The search result includes a granted patent and a related application. Can we treat both as the same right?
  12. Arun: No. Verify the legal status and actual claims for each relevant record. Related filings can differ in scope and procedural position.
  13. Mei: We should also make clear that the report is about market A, not every country in the sales forecast.
  14. Arun: Exactly. The territory of interest matters, along with the specified activities and the applicable law.
  15. Mei: Management would like a short answer before the launch meeting, even if several factual questions remain unresolved.
  16. Arun: We can give a concise status, but not label it a completed clearance opinion while the configuration and relevant rights are still under review.
  17. Mei: I will replace clear to sell with review ongoing and identify the missing drawing as the engineering team's next action.
  18. Arun: That is accurate. The eventual assessment should also explain any residual risk within its stated scope rather than promising that no claim can ever arise.
  19. Mei: Once the design is reviewed, I will flag any further connector or channel change before relying on the same conclusion.
  20. Arun: Make that a review trigger. A scoped assessment stays useful only when the team knows when its underlying facts have changed.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The redesigned housing is not merely cosmetic

Counsel checks a production change.

  1. Engineer: We changed the housing for manufacturing, but the sales team says it is only a cosmetic update.
  2. Counsel: The label is not enough. What components and relationships changed in the actual production configuration?
  3. Engineer: The channel now connects through a different fitting, and the flow path is slightly longer.
  4. Counsel: Please provide the controlled drawing and a comparison with the version previously reviewed.
  5. Engineer: That will let you decide whether the earlier assessment needs to be revisited.
  6. Counsel: Yes. Do not assume the conclusion applies automatically simply because the product name stayed the same.
  7. Engineer: I will also identify the intended market and commercial activity in the request.
  8. Counsel: Those details help define the relevant scope instead of asking for an unbounded assurance.
  9. Engineer: The launch note will say the changed configuration is awaiting the appropriate review.
  10. Counsel: That keeps a manufacturing update from silently becoming an unsupported clearance conclusion.
An expired record does not settle the family

A product lead asks about search status.

  1. Lead: One database entry says expired. Can we remove the entire patent family from the review?
  2. Counsel: Not on that observation alone. We need the relevant records, territory, claims, and current legal status.
  3. Lead: The grouped results include another application that appears to remain pending.
  4. Counsel: Then distinguish the individual documents rather than applying one member's status to every related filing.
  5. Lead: I will send the exact identifiers and the date of the database check.
  6. Counsel: Good. The responsible review can then verify the actual information and its significance.
  7. Lead: We should also avoid treating a search database label as a complete legal opinion.
  8. Counsel: Correct. It is a source to assess, not a substitute for the scoped analysis.
  9. Lead: The revised table will retain the family relationship while separating each material status.
  10. Counsel: That makes the finding usable without erasing a potentially relevant record through overgeneralization.
The opinion covered manufacture, not every transaction

Counsel clarifies an assessment request.

  1. Manager: The earlier report reviewed manufacturing in A. Sales now wants to rely on it for imports into B.
  2. Counsel: Those are different activities and territories, so we need to check the actual opinion scope.
  3. Manager: The product configuration is unchanged, but the proposed commercial route is new.
  4. Counsel: That still matters. An unchanged design does not automatically make every legal question identical.
  5. Manager: I will supply the intended activities and locations rather than ask whether the product is simply safe.
  6. Counsel: Please do. A specific request allows the relevant rights and assumptions to be assessed.
  7. Manager: The earlier conclusion should remain attached to its stated facts and limitations.
  8. Counsel: Exactly. Do not remove those qualifications when preparing the commercial summary.
  9. Manager: I will mark the new route as requiring review and identify the business decision it supports.
  10. Counsel: That gives the team a practical next step without expanding the old opinion beyond its basis.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Our own patent does not answer the launch question
  1. Read the corrected exchange and distinguish patentability, ownership, and freedom to operate.
  2. Switch roles and restate the missing information without promising that the connector change avoids any claim.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The sales team adds another market

A completed assessment concerns one specified product in market A. Sales now proposes market B, which is outside that assessment.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Sales: "We want to add another ___."

Answer and explanation

C. market

The proposed change is expansion to a different geographic market.

2. Counsel: "B is outside the existing review ___."

Answer and explanation

C. scope

The supplied assessment concerns market A only, not market B.

3. Sales: "We need the relevant local rights ___."

Answer and explanation

D. checked

The new territory requires the appropriate rights assessment rather than automatic reuse.

4. Counsel: "Do not describe the earlier opinion as ___ clearance."

Answer and explanation

D. worldwide

The prior assessment is expressly limited and cannot support universal coverage.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Our own patent does not settle third-party rights. / Which product version and market does the assessment cover? / Please provide the revised drawings before we assess the change. / A different appearance does not by itself establish a design-around. / We need the current claims and legal status. / Any opinion must state its scope, assumptions, and limits. / A material design change should trigger reassessment. / Clearance is under review, not already confirmed. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: freedom to operate: Assessment of relevant rights affecting specified commercial activity in a territory. / right to exclude: Patent right to prevent specified acts within its legal scope. / third-party patent: Patent belonging to another party. / commercial clearance: Qualified assessment addressing specified launch or commercial risks. / product configuration: Particular design and components being evaluated. / territory of interest: Geographic jurisdiction relevant to the proposed activity. / legal status: Current procedural or rights status of a patent or application. / claim chart: Structured comparison of claim requirements with relevant features. / literal infringement: Infringement analysis based on the claim requirements under applicable interpretation. / doctrine of equivalents: Jurisdiction-specific doctrine addressing certain equivalents beyond literal wording. / design-around: Product change intended to avoid relevant patent scope, requiring assessment. / blocking patent: Patent whose relevant scope may constrain proposed commercial activity. / expired patent: Patent whose term or rights have ended subject to actual legal status. / pending application: Application not yet resolved by the relevant final status. / continuing application: Related further application under an applicable filing system. / clearance opinion: Professional opinion within a defined commercial-clearance scope. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the stated review or action. / review trigger: Change or event requiring reassessment. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Negotiating a patent-license term sheetExclusive in one field is not exclusive everywhere+

Your goal

Clarify grant scope, economic terms, and unresolved conditions during a licensing discussion.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Exclusive in one field is not exclusive everywhere

Licensor representative Nia and prospective licensee Ben review a draft term sheet. It proposes exclusivity for industrial cooling in territory A, with other fields excluded. A royalty percentage is discussed, but the net-sales definition and treatment of returns are unresolved. Sublicensing requires a separately negotiated provision. No agreement has been executed.

Ben: Prospective licensee / Nia: Licensor representative

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What exclusivity is proposed?

Answer and explanation

D. Industrial cooling in territory A

The draft limits proposed exclusivity to the named field and territory.

2. Which economic issue remains unresolved?

Answer and explanation

A. Net-sales definition and returns treatment

The royalty base and return treatment are open even though a percentage has been discussed.

3. What is the agreement status?

Answer and explanation

B. Unexecuted draft terms

The brief states that no agreement has been executed and important terms remain open.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Exclusive must be read with its field, territory, rights, and conditions. A percentage has limited meaning without the agreed royalty base.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
licensor
Party granting rights under a license.identify the licensor
licensee
Party receiving rights under a license.identify the licensee
license grant
Provision specifying licensed rights and limits.negotiate the license grant
exclusive license
License granting exclusivity as defined by the actual agreement.define the exclusive license
nonexclusive license
License without the specified exclusivity.distinguish a nonexclusive license
field of use
Defined application area within a license.restrict the field of use
licensed territory
Geographic scope of licensed rights.define the licensed territory
sublicense
Permission granted onward under rights allowed by the primary license.negotiate sublicense rights
royalty base
Amount or measure to which the royalty rate applies.define the royalty base
net sales
Contract-defined sales measure after specified adjustments.negotiate net sales
upfront payment
Payment due at the initial contractual stage.agree an upfront payment
milestone payment
Payment linked to a specified event or achievement.define a milestone payment
minimum royalty
Contractual minimum payment under the stated terms.negotiate a minimum royalty
audit right
Contractual ability to verify relevant records.agree audit rights
improvement rights
Contractual treatment of later improvements.clarify improvement rights
termination trigger
Event allowing or requiring termination under the agreement.define a termination trigger
term sheet
Document recording proposed or preliminary commercial terms.revise the term sheet
execution
Formal signing or completion under the applicable agreement process.confirm execution
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

A term sheet can have legal consequences depending on its actual wording and law. This fictional draft is not executed, and no binding effect or negotiation protection is determined.

C Notice the language

Narrow the disagreement

Negotiations move forward when each side identifies the specific right or economic assumption it needs. Avoid treating a headline percentage as a complete commercial agreement.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify exclusivity
Exclusive for which field and territory?
Define the economics
Which deductions are included in the net-sales definition?
Separate rate and base
We have discussed the percentage, not settled the amount it applies to.
Check onward rights
Sublicensing needs its own agreed provision.
Trade a specific term
We can discuss that minimum alongside the defined commercial commitment.
Keep versions aligned
Please mark the revised grant language in the next draft.
Preserve open items
The royalty base and returns treatment remain unresolved.
Confirm status
These are proposed terms, not an executed license.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Exclusive versus unrestricted
Exclusivity can be limited to a defined field or territory and does not imply unlimited rights.
Rate versus base
Rate is the percentage or multiplier; base is the measure to which it applies.
Agreed in principle versus executed
Preliminary agreement on a point and formal completion are distinct statuses; actual legal effect requires review.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which description matches the proposed exclusivity?

Answer and explanation

C. Industrial cooling in A, subject to final terms

The supplied proposal is limited by field and territory and remains unexecuted.

2. Why is the percentage alone insufficient?

Answer and explanation

B. The royalty base and return treatment are unresolved

The amount payable depends on the agreed base and adjustments, not only the rate.

3. Which request requires separate negotiation here?

Answer and explanation

A. Permission to sublicense

The brief states that sublicensing requires a separately negotiated provision.

4. Which closing summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Proposed grant discussed; economics and sublicensing remain open

This summary preserves the specific unresolved terms without assuming execution or legal effect.

E Conversations

Exclusive in one field is not exclusive everywhere

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Prospective licensee / Nia: Licensor representative

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • field of use
  • audit right
  • net sales
  • term sheet
  • milestone payment
  • improvement rights
  • sublicense
  • royalty base
  • execution
  • licensed territory
  1. Ben
    The heading says exclusive license. Our sales team reads that as permission to use the technology in any product we develop.
  2. Nia
    The proposed 1 is industrial cooling. The heading must be read with that limitation and the rest of the grant.
  3. Ben
    We also plan to sell in market B. I assumed the same exclusivity would follow the product wherever it travels.
  4. Nia
    The draft 2 is A. Any request to include B needs explicit negotiation rather than an assumption from the word exclusive.
  5. Ben
    Understood. We have discussed the percentage, but our finance team cannot calculate the expected payment from the current wording.
  6. Nia
    We still need to settle the 3, including which sales and adjustments the definition actually covers.
  7. Ben
    Returns matter for our business. We need the calculation to distinguish invoiced sales from products later returned by customers.
  8. Nia
    That belongs in the 4 definition. We should agree the treatment and timing rather than rely on an undefined accounting shorthand.
  9. Ben
    A regional partner would distribute the product and may want rights to manufacture a version for local customers.
  10. Nia
    Distribution and a 5 are not interchangeable. Any onward manufacturing rights need the appropriate negotiated provision.
  11. Ben
    Could we discuss a lower initial payment if we accept a payment when the first approved product enters commercial production?
  12. Nia
    We can discuss a 6, but the event, evidence, and payment obligation would need precise agreement.
  13. Ben
    Our finance team also wants to know what records we would need to maintain for royalty verification.
  14. Nia
    The proposed 7 should address the relevant records and conditions, with appropriate confidentiality and practical arrangements.
  15. Ben
    We expect to develop improved connectors. I do not want either side to assume ownership or access without discussing it.
  16. Nia
    Then include 8 as a separate open item, alongside the licensed patents and any other defined subject matter.
  17. Ben
    I will send a marked-up version showing our requests on territory, returns, sublicensing, and later improvements.
  18. Nia
    Please keep the 9 status clear. A discussion of those points does not mean the license has been completed.
  19. Ben
    Our closing summary should say that the commercial proposal is progressing, with several specified provisions still unresolved.
  20. Nia
    Agreed. Do not describe 10 as complete. We need the final terms, proper review, and authorized completion before reporting an executed license.

Answers and explanations
  1. field of use Turn 2. The field of use defines the permitted application area and prevents exclusive from being mistaken for unrestricted use.
  2. licensed territory Turn 4. The licensed territory defines geographic scope, which does not automatically expand with the licensee sales plans.
  3. royalty base Turn 6. The royalty base is the amount or measure to which the percentage applies, so an agreed rate alone does not determine payment.
  4. net sales Turn 8. Net sales is a contract-defined measure whose deductions and adjustments need explicit agreement rather than assumed standard treatment.
  5. sublicense Turn 10. A sublicense grants onward rights only as permitted by the primary agreement and must not be inferred from distribution arrangements alone.
  6. milestone payment Turn 12. A milestone payment depends on a specifically defined event, which needs precise terms rather than an informal expectation of progress.
  7. audit right Turn 14. An audit right defines contractual verification access and conditions, which should be agreed rather than assumed from the royalty obligation alone.
  8. improvement rights Turn 16. Improvement rights address contractual treatment of later developments and must not be inferred from the original license heading.
  9. term sheet Turn 18. The term sheet records the proposed terms and open issues; its actual legal effect depends on its wording and applicable law.
  10. execution Turn 20. Execution refers to formal completion or signing under the actual agreement process, which has not occurred in the supplied case.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: The heading says exclusive license. Our sales team reads that as permission to use the technology in any product we develop.
  2. Nia: The proposed field of use is industrial cooling. The heading must be read with that limitation and the rest of the grant.
  3. Ben: We also plan to sell in market B. I assumed the same exclusivity would follow the product wherever it travels.
  4. Nia: The draft licensed territory is A. Any request to include B needs explicit negotiation rather than an assumption from the word exclusive.
  5. Ben: Understood. We have discussed the percentage, but our finance team cannot calculate the expected payment from the current wording.
  6. Nia: We still need to settle the royalty base, including which sales and adjustments the definition actually covers.
  7. Ben: Returns matter for our business. We need the calculation to distinguish invoiced sales from products later returned by customers.
  8. Nia: That belongs in the net sales definition. We should agree the treatment and timing rather than rely on an undefined accounting shorthand.
  9. Ben: A regional partner would distribute the product and may want rights to manufacture a version for local customers.
  10. Nia: Distribution and a sublicense are not interchangeable. Any onward manufacturing rights need the appropriate negotiated provision.
  11. Ben: Could we discuss a lower initial payment if we accept a payment when the first approved product enters commercial production?
  12. Nia: We can discuss a milestone payment, but the event, evidence, and payment obligation would need precise agreement.
  13. Ben: Our finance team also wants to know what records we would need to maintain for royalty verification.
  14. Nia: The proposed audit right should address the relevant records and conditions, with appropriate confidentiality and practical arrangements.
  15. Ben: We expect to develop improved connectors. I do not want either side to assume ownership or access without discussing it.
  16. Nia: Then include improvement rights as a separate open item, alongside the licensed patents and any other defined subject matter.
  17. Ben: I will send a marked-up version showing our requests on territory, returns, sublicensing, and later improvements.
  18. Nia: Please keep the term sheet status clear. A discussion of those points does not mean the license has been completed.
  19. Ben: Our closing summary should say that the commercial proposal is progressing, with several specified provisions still unresolved.
  20. Nia: Agreed. Do not describe execution as complete. We need the final terms, proper review, and authorized completion before reporting an executed license.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Distributor rights are not manufacturing rights

Two negotiators clarify a proposed license.

  1. Licensee: Our distributor wants to manufacture a local version, so we assumed the distribution arrangement covers that too.
  2. Licensor: Those activities need to be distinguished against the actual rights proposed in the license.
  3. Licensee: The draft currently describes resale of finished products, not an onward manufacturing permission.
  4. Licensor: Then we should raise the manufacturing request explicitly rather than treat it as an implied extension.
  5. Licensee: We also need to identify the relevant territory and whether a sublicense would be required.
  6. Licensor: Yes. The parties should negotiate the actual scope and conditions with the appropriate legal review.
  7. Licensee: I will add the request as an open item in the marked-up term sheet.
  8. Licensor: Please keep the existing proposal and the requested expansion separately visible.
  9. Licensee: Our commercial summary will not say the distributor already has those rights.
  10. Licensor: That avoids converting a business preference into a permission that has not been granted.
A milestone needs an observable trigger

Negotiators discuss a payment provision.

  1. Licensee: The draft says payment is due when development is substantially complete, but our teams interpret that differently.
  2. Licensor: Then the trigger needs a more precise definition rather than relying on a shared-sounding phrase.
  3. Licensee: Our engineering group means completion of the prototype, while finance assumes commercial production has begun.
  4. Licensor: Those are materially different events and could lead to different payment dates.
  5. Licensee: We should identify the agreed event and the evidence used to confirm it.
  6. Licensor: Yes, together with the actual payment terms and any review process the parties agree.
  7. Licensee: I will not replace the phrase unilaterally with our preferred interpretation.
  8. Licensor: Good. Mark the proposed wording so both sides can assess the change.
  9. Licensee: The term sheet should keep that payment provision open until the issue is resolved.
  10. Licensor: That makes the remaining negotiation concrete instead of hiding it inside an ambiguous milestone label.
Improvements are a separate negotiation

A licensee raises a development concern.

  1. Licensee: Our team may develop a new connector, and we want to know how later improvements would be treated.
  2. Licensor: The current heading does not settle ownership, access, or licensing of those later developments.
  3. Licensee: We should identify the particular rights each side is requesting rather than use improvements as one broad label.
  4. Licensor: Agreed. The definitions and obligations need to be clear enough for both technical and commercial teams.
  5. Licensee: We also want to distinguish our existing background technology from work developed during the relationship.
  6. Licensor: That distinction belongs in the actual negotiated provisions, with appropriate legal review.
  7. Licensee: I will provide a marked proposal showing those separate categories and the requested treatment.
  8. Licensor: Please keep proposed terms separate from rights already held or granted under another agreement.
  9. Licensee: The next meeting can address the identified differences without assuming that exclusivity answers everything.
  10. Licensor: Exactly. A precise open-item list is more useful than a broad assurance neither side intended to give.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Exclusive in one field is not exclusive everywhere
  1. Read the corrected negotiation, stressing field, territory, rate, and base.
  2. Switch roles and list the four requested revisions without treating proposed terms as completed rights.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The rate is unchanged but the base differs

Two draft royalty provisions use the same percentage. Draft A permits specified returns deductions; draft B does not. Neither is approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The two drafts use the same ___."

Answer and explanation

C. rate

The supplied percentage is identical in both draft provisions.

2. Counsel: "Their royalty ___ differs."

Answer and explanation

D. base

The return deduction changes the amount to which the rate applies.

3. Analyst: "We must compare the permitted ___."

Answer and explanation

D. deductions

The stated difference concerns whether returns may reduce the calculation.

4. Counsel: "Both provisions remain ___."

Answer and explanation

C. proposed

Neither draft has been approved, so neither is a completed agreed term.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.
