Dialogue 05: The examiner raised two different issues
Original fictional setting: A fictional US non-final office action rejects claim 1 over Reference R and separately questions the clarity of the term substantially aligned. The client wants to say the examiner rejected the product. Counsel Elise proposes reviewing the cited mapping and the unclear term before selecting a response. The actual notice controls all dates; no deadline or guaranteed outcome is supplied.
Professional roles: Jon / Elise
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Jon: The examiner rejected our product. Management wants a quick assurance that your reply will reverse that decision.
Elise: The office action addresses the claims and their wording, not whether the product can be sold or will succeed commercially.
Jon: The notice points to Reference R for claim 1 and also questions the phrase substantially aligned.
Elise: Those are separate issues. We need to address the ground of rejection and examine the clarity concern rather than assume one answer resolves both.
Jon: Our engineer thinks the channel in R runs beside the housing instead of through the relationship described in our draft.
Elise: That may identify a distinguishing feature. Please show the relevant passages and explain the technical relationship precisely.
Jon: We can provide a side-by-side diagram, but it should not introduce details that are missing from the filed application.
Elise: Correct. Any proposed claim amendment needs support and scope review, while our argument must stay consistent with the actual disclosure.
Jon: Could we first ask the examiner how that passage is being read instead of guessing the intended mapping?
Elise: We can assess an examiner interview under the applicable procedure. A discussion may clarify the issue without guaranteeing agreement.
Jon: If the conversation sounds encouraging, may I tell management that the application has been approved?
Elise: No. An encouraging discussion is not allowance. We need an accurate record of what was discussed and the actual subsequent procedural status.
Jon: I will describe the response as proposed until we have reviewed the alternatives and authorized the next step.
Elise: That preserves client instruction. We should explain the consequences of an amendment and an argument before confirming the chosen response.
Jon: The notice is labeled non-final. Does that mean we can leave it until the next quarterly engineering meeting?
Elise: No. Verify the response deadline and tracking record promptly; the label is not permission to disregard timing.
Jon: I also want the team to understand that a formal response becomes part of the examination record.
Elise: Yes. The prosecution history matters, so we should review technical explanations and legal arguments carefully before submitting them.
Jon: I will send the mapping and wording comments, then review your proposed alternatives with the authorized client contact.
Elise: We can then agree on the response strategy, keeping the notice, instructions, deadline, and eventual filing confirmation separate in the status report.
Vocabulary in this exchange: claim: Statement defining the subject matter for which protection is sought or granted. / amendment: Proposed or entered change to application documents. / office action: Official communication from a patent examiner or office. / ground of rejection: Stated legal basis for rejecting a claim. / response deadline: Time limit governing the required response. / claim amendment: Proposed or entered change to claim wording.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 06: An international report is not a worldwide grant
Original fictional setting: Coordinator Hana has received an international search report and written opinion for a PCT application. Manager Luis calls it a world patent. The company is evaluating markets A, B, and C but has authorized none for the national or regional stage. Local requirements, costs, translations, and actual deadlines remain for verification with the responsible professionals.
Professional roles: Luis / Hana
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Luis: The search report has arrived. Can I tell the board that our world patent now covers markets A, B, and C?
Hana: Please call it an international application under the PCT. We do not have a worldwide grant from receiving those reports.
Luis: I see an international search report and a separate assessment discussing the claims. Do they perform the same function?
Hana: The written opinion gives an assessment under that procedure; neither document itself grants patents in the markets on our list.
Luis: The reports will still help us decide whether the commercial opportunity justifies further work in each market.
Hana: Yes. They inform the portfolio review, alongside business priorities and qualified advice on the actual findings.
Luis: The market slide lists A, B, and C. I assumed that putting them on the slide authorized your next steps.
Hana: We need a specific filing instruction. Evaluation and authorization are different statuses, especially where costs and timing are involved.
Luis: Should the board expect exactly the same requirements in each place once the international stage is complete?
Hana: No. The relevant national requirements or regional requirements must be checked with the responsible professionals using current information.
Luis: I have three estimates, but one includes translation and another mentions only the local professional fee.
Hana: Then the jurisdiction budget comparison needs clearer assumptions before anyone treats those totals as equivalent.
Luis: We also need to know who checks the language and format of each version, not just who places the order.
Hana: Confirm the translation specification and review responsibility. A completed translation is not proof that every filing requirement has been met.
Luis: The local firms can confirm the requirements and actual dates, but the central team must send consistent instructions.
Hana: Each local associate should receive the authorized scope, documents, and a request for confirmation of the action taken.
Luis: Once the national-stage actions are completed, should the status report say protection is granted in each place?
Hana: No. Entry and the grant decision are separate. Report each application or right using its actual territory and procedural status.
Luis: I will revise the board slide to show reports received, market decisions pending, and local requirements under review.
Hana: Good. That explains territorial protection without promising grants, inventing deadlines, or treating the application as permission to commercialize everywhere.
Vocabulary in this exchange: specification: Written disclosure forming part of a patent application. / international application: Application filed under the relevant international procedure. / international search report: Report identifying relevant search results in the PCT process. / written opinion: Written international-stage assessment under the relevant PCT procedure. / national requirements: Requirements applicable before the relevant national office. / translation specification: Requirements for the language version needed in a filing.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: Our own patent does not answer the launch question
Original fictional setting: Product lead Mei says the company can launch its enclosure because it owns a patent. Counsel Arun explains that a separate freedom-to-operate review concerns third-party rights and the actual product configuration in market A. The engineers propose moving a connector but have not supplied revised drawings. No clearance opinion, infringement finding, or design-around conclusion has been issued.
Professional roles: Mei / Arun
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Mei: We own a patent on the enclosure, so I told the launch team that the product is clear to sell in market A.
Arun: Ownership gives us a right to exclude within the patent scope. It does not settle whether third-party rights affect our product.
Mei: Then the patentability search we commissioned earlier answered a different question from the one the launch team is asking now.
Arun: Correct. A freedom to operate review needs a defined product, activity, territory, and current legal information.
Mei: Our first market is A, and the production design has changed since the version used in the earlier review.
Arun: Please identify the current product configuration rather than assuming that an earlier drawing still represents what will be sold.
Mei: Engineering proposes moving the connector to the opposite side. The team thinks that should avoid the competitor patent.
Arun: We need the revised drawings before assessing that design-around. A different appearance does not by itself answer the claim-scope question.
Mei: I can obtain a controlled drawing and a list of changed components, with the old version retained for comparison.
Arun: Good. We can use a claim chart to organize the relevant comparison, subject to proper claim interpretation and legal analysis.
Mei: The search result includes a granted patent and a related application. Can we treat both as the same right?
Arun: No. Verify the legal status and actual claims for each relevant record. Related filings can differ in scope and procedural position.
Mei: We should also make clear that the report is about market A, not every country in the sales forecast.
Arun: Exactly. The territory of interest matters, along with the specified activities and the applicable law.
Mei: Management would like a short answer before the launch meeting, even if several factual questions remain unresolved.
Arun: We can give a concise status, but not label it a completed clearance opinion while the configuration and relevant rights are still under review.
Mei: I will replace clear to sell with review ongoing and identify the missing drawing as the engineering team's next action.
Arun: That is accurate. The eventual assessment should also explain any residual risk within its stated scope rather than promising that no claim can ever arise.
Mei: Once the design is reviewed, I will flag any further connector or channel change before relying on the same conclusion.
Arun: Make that a review trigger. A scoped assessment stays useful only when the team knows when its underlying facts have changed.
Vocabulary in this exchange: claim: Statement defining the subject matter for which protection is sought or granted. / freedom to operate: Assessment of relevant rights affecting specified commercial activity in a territory. / right to exclude: Patent right to prevent specified acts within its legal scope. / product configuration: Particular design and components being evaluated. / territory of interest: Geographic jurisdiction relevant to the proposed activity. / legal status: Current procedural or rights status of a patent or application.
END REFERENCE

Professional roles: Mei / Arun
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Mei: We own a patent on the enclosure, so I told the launch team that the product is clear to sell in market A.
Arun: Ownership gives us a right to exclude within the patent scope. It does not settle whether third-party rights affect our product.
Mei: Then the patentability search we commissioned earlier answered a different question from the one the launch team is asking now.
Arun: Correct. A freedom to operate review needs a defined product, activity, territory, and current legal information.
Mei: Our first market is A, and the production design has changed since the version used in the earlier review.
Arun: Please identify the current product configuration rather than assuming that an earlier drawing still represents what will be sold.
Mei: Engineering proposes moving the connector to the opposite side. The team thinks that should avoid the competitor patent.
Arun: We need the revised drawings before assessing that design-around. A different appearance does not by itself answer the claim-scope question.
Mei: I can obtain a controlled drawing and a list of changed components, with the old version retained for comparison.
Arun: Good. We can use a claim chart to organize the relevant comparison, subject to proper claim interpretation and legal analysis.
Mei: The search result includes a granted patent and a related application. Can we treat both as the same right?
Arun: No. Verify the legal status and actual claims for each relevant record. Related filings can differ in scope and procedural position.
Mei: We should also make clear that the report is about market A, not every country in the sales forecast.
Arun: Exactly. The territory of interest matters, along with the specified activities and the applicable law.
Mei: Management would like a short answer before the launch meeting, even if several factual questions remain unresolved.
Arun: We can give a concise status, but not label it a completed clearance opinion while the configuration and relevant rights are still under review.
Mei: I will replace clear to sell with review ongoing and identify the missing drawing as the engineering team's next action.
Arun: That is accurate. The eventual assessment should also explain any residual risk within its stated scope rather than promising that no claim can ever arise.
Mei: Once the design is reviewed, I will flag any further connector or channel change before relying on the same conclusion.
Arun: Make that a review trigger. A scoped assessment stays useful only when the team knows when its underlying facts have changed.
Vocabulary in this exchange: claim: Statement defining the subject matter for which protection is sought or granted. / freedom to operate: Assessment of relevant rights affecting specified commercial activity in a territory. / right to exclude: Patent right to prevent specified acts within its legal scope. / product configuration: Particular design and components being evaluated. / territory of interest: Geographic jurisdiction relevant to the proposed activity. / legal status: Current procedural or rights status of a patent or application.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 08: Exclusive in one field is not exclusive everywhere
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Dialogue 08: Exclusive in one field is not exclusive everywhere
Original fictional setting: Licensor representative Nia and prospective licensee Ben review a draft term sheet. It proposes exclusivity for industrial cooling in territory A, with other fields excluded. A royalty percentage is discussed, but the net-sales definition and treatment of returns are unresolved. Sublicensing requires a separately negotiated provision. No agreement has been executed.
Professional roles: Ben / Nia
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Ben: The heading says exclusive license. Our sales team reads that as permission to use the technology in any product we develop.
Nia: The proposed field of use is industrial cooling. The heading must be read with that limitation and the rest of the grant.
Ben: We also plan to sell in market B. I assumed the same exclusivity would follow the product wherever it travels.
Nia: The draft licensed territory is A. Any request to include B needs explicit negotiation rather than an assumption from the word exclusive.
Ben: Understood. We have discussed the percentage, but our finance team cannot calculate the expected payment from the current wording.
Nia: We still need to settle the royalty base, including which sales and adjustments the definition actually covers.
Ben: Returns matter for our business. We need the calculation to distinguish invoiced sales from products later returned by customers.
Nia: That belongs in the net sales definition. We should agree the treatment and timing rather than rely on an undefined accounting shorthand.
Ben: A regional partner would distribute the product and may want rights to manufacture a version for local customers.
Nia: Distribution and a sublicense are not interchangeable. Any onward manufacturing rights need the appropriate negotiated provision.
Ben: Could we discuss a lower initial payment if we accept a payment when the first approved product enters commercial production?
Nia: We can discuss a milestone payment, but the event, evidence, and payment obligation would need precise agreement.
Ben: Our finance team also wants to know what records we would need to maintain for royalty verification.
Nia: The proposed audit right should address the relevant records and conditions, with appropriate confidentiality and practical arrangements.
Ben: We expect to develop improved connectors. I do not want either side to assume ownership or access without discussing it.
Nia: Then include improvement rights as a separate open item, alongside the licensed patents and any other defined subject matter.
Ben: I will send a marked-up version showing our requests on territory, returns, sublicensing, and later improvements.
Nia: Please keep the term sheet status clear. A discussion of those points does not mean the license has been completed.
Ben: Our closing summary should say that the commercial proposal is progressing, with several specified provisions still unresolved.
Nia: Agreed. Do not describe execution as complete. We need the final terms, proper review, and authorized completion before reporting an executed license.
Vocabulary in this exchange: exclusive license: License granting exclusivity as defined by the actual agreement. / field of use: Defined application area within a license. / licensed territory: Geographic scope of licensed rights. / sublicense: Permission granted onward under rights allowed by the primary license. / royalty base: Amount or measure to which the royalty rate applies. / net sales: Contract-defined sales measure after specified adjustments.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

REFERENCE
Course: Patent Law English
Professional audience: patent attorneys, patent agents, intellectual-property specialists, invention reviewers
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. Language practice, not patent or legal advice. Examples simplify documents and do not establish inventorship, patentability, validity, infringement, priority, filing deadlines, licensing rights, or freedom to operate. Real matters require qualified patent professionals, current law and office practice, and the actual documents. Protect confidential inventions and observe applicable filing, disclosure, security, and export requirements.
Lesson 01: Interviewing inventors without leading their answers
Original fictional case: Engineer Amira describes a prototype battery enclosure with a curved coolant channel. Technician Leo built it from drawings and suggested a detachable connector. One internal test reports a shorter cooling time, but the test conditions have not been compared. Patent professional Daniel is gathering facts, not deciding inventorship or patentability.
Language workshop: The improvement is a channel, not simply faster cooling
Communication goal: Elicit the mechanism, alternatives, and individual contributions behind an invention disclosure.
Language guidance: Describe each contribution before assessing its legal significance. Building a prototype and conceiving a claimed feature are not interchangeable descriptions.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What does the curved channel change in the flow path? / Would the same effect occur with a straight channel? / Who first proposed that feature, and what records support the account? / The test reports a shorter time; it does not yet establish why. / Did Leo follow the drawings or propose a different arrangement? / We have not compared the test conditions yet. / Please use the approved confidential channel for the drawings. / We have a prototype, a reported result, and contribution details to verify. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: invention disclosure: Internal account of a proposed invention and its development. / technical problem: Practical technical difficulty the proposed invention addresses. / technical effect: Technical result attributed to a feature or process. / conception: Formation of the inventive idea under the applicable legal test. / reduction to practice: Implementation or legally relevant realization of an invention. / inventorship: Legal identification of the inventor or inventors. / contribution record: Account of who contributed what and when. / prototype: Preliminary implementation used to explore or test a design. / embodiment: Particular implementation described for an invention. / alternative configuration: Different arrangement of components or steps. / coolant channel: Passage through which cooling fluid moves. / detachable connector: Connection component designed to be separated. / causal mechanism: Process explaining how a feature produces a result. / comparative test: Test comparing implementations under stated conditions. / baseline configuration: Reference arrangement used for comparison. / laboratory notebook: Contemporaneous record of experimental work. / confidential disclosure: Communication within the relevant confidentiality arrangements. / public disclosure: Making information available outside the relevant confidential setting. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected interview and stress designed, built, suggested, and tested where their meanings differ. Switch roles and deliver the final document list using only the supplied development facts. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Amira: Engineer / Daniel: Patent professional
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A second enclosure appears only in a dated sketch. It has not been built or tested. The team wants to include it in the confidential disclosure.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Amira: Marketing wants to show the prototype at a public event next month. I have not approved that request.
Daniel: Flag the proposed public disclosure for prompt legal review before anyone assumes the timing is harmless.
Amira: I will supply the drawings, contribution details, test conditions, and event request as separate items for your review.
Daniel: That gives the invention disclosure a factual foundation without promising protection or treating an initial result as a verified product claim.
END REFERENCE
